CAT-VARC-P02-001 · Perception, attention and memory · passage Medium · 483 words · 4 questions

It is natural to think of seeing as a kind of recording. Light enters the eye, an image forms on the retina, and the brain, like a careful clerk, reads off what is there. The picture is attractive because it matches how perception feels: effortless, immediate, and apparently free of any contribution from the perceiver. Yet the retinal image is a poor document: it is upside down, flat, constantly jittering with the movements of the eye, and interrupted by a blind spot where the optic nerve leaves the retina. Above all, it is ambiguous, in the sense that a small image on the retina could be a small object nearby or a large one far away; a patch of dim grey could be a dark surface in bright light or a pale surface in shadow. The eye, in short, delivers considerably less information than we ordinarily experience ourselves as seeing.

For this reason many students of vision have preferred to describe perception as a form of inference. The brain, on this account, treats the incoming signal as evidence and settles on the interpretation that best explains it, given what it has learned about how the world usually is. Light tends to come from above, so a disc shaded darker at the bottom is seen as a bump and the same disc turned over as a dent. Objects tend to keep their size, so a person walking away is seen as receding rather than shrinking. These are not conclusions we reach; they are conclusions reached on our behalf, before anything is presented to awareness.

The word 'inference' invites an obvious objection: if perception is a guess shaped by expectation, why do we not simply see whatever we expect? The answer is that the guess is tightly disciplined by the evidence, and that the expectations doing the work are of a peculiar kind. They are not the beliefs we hold and can revise in conversation, but assumptions built up over a lifetime of looking, and perhaps over the history of the species. This is why many visual illusions survive full knowledge of the trick. A viewer who has measured two lines and found them equal will still see one as longer; what she knows and what she sees have been settled by different processes.

It would be a mistake, then, to conclude that perception is unreliable because it is inferential. The assumptions it relies on are mostly true of the environments in which they were formed, which is why they so rarely mislead us outside the laboratory, where illusions are carefully built to exploit them. What the inferential view really overturns is not our trust in the senses but the idea that seeing is passive. Every glance is a small act of interpretation, and its seeming effortlessness is the mark of how well the work is done, not evidence that no work is being done.

CAT-VARC-P02-001-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)explain how the defects of the retinal image, such as its blind spot, are corrected by the beliefs a viewer holds about the world.
  2. (B)defend the view that seeing is a form of conscious reasoning in which the brain, like a careful clerk, weighs the evidence.
  3. (C)show that visual illusions reveal how unreliable perception is, so that what we see should be checked against measurement.
  4. (D)argue that seeing actively interprets ambiguous evidence, while denying that this makes the senses untrustworthy.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 rejects the picture of seeing as recording, paragraph 2 offers the inferential account, paragraph 3 answers an objection to it, and paragraph 4 concludes that the view overturns 'the idea that seeing is passive' but not 'our trust in the senses'. The correct answer holds both the thesis and the qualification.

Why not (A): The blind spot is one detail of paragraph 1, and paragraph 3 says the expectations at work are not 'the beliefs we hold and can revise', so the option is both too narrow and a distortion.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says these are 'not conclusions we reach', and the careful clerk belongs to the recording picture the author rejects in paragraph 1.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says it would be 'a mistake' to conclude that perception is unreliable, and that illusions are built in laboratories to exploit assumptions that rarely mislead us elsewhere.

Common misconception: Picking the illusion option because illusions are discussed at length, and missing that the author uses them to make a different point.

Difficulty: One wrong option turns the author's concession into its opposite; another borrows the clerk image from the view being rejected. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-001-Q2 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, the retinal image is ambiguous in the sense that
  1. (A)eye movements make it change from moment to moment, so that no single image can ever be relied on.
  2. (B)it is upside down and flat, so the brain has to turn it the right way up before anything can be seen at all.
  3. (C)the same pattern on the retina is consistent with more than one arrangement of things in the world.
  4. (D)the blind spot removes part of every image, leaving gaps that the viewer's expectations must fill in.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 gives two illustrations of ambiguity: a small retinal image 'could be a small object nearby or a large one far away', and dim grey 'could be a dark surface in bright light or a pale surface in shadow'. In each, one image fits two states of the world.

Why not (A): Jitter is mentioned as a separate defect, and the passage never says that no image can be relied on.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 lists inversion and flatness as separate defects before 'Above all, it is ambiguous'; ambiguity is illustrated by the size and grey patch examples, not by inversion.

Why not (D): The blind spot is another of the listed defects; the passage illustrates ambiguity with one image fitting two different scenes, not with missing parts of the image.

Common misconception: Choosing one of the other listed defects of the retinal image instead of the one the passage calls ambiguity.

Difficulty: The answer is in two adjacent sentences; the wrong options are the other defects in the same list. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-001-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
The author explains that perceptual assumptions rarely mislead us outside the laboratory because they are mostly true of the environments in which they were formed. Which of the following, if true, would most weaken this explanation?
  1. (A)People who move into surroundings that violate their perceptual assumptions are, from the start, misled no more often than people who grew up there.
  2. (B)Laboratory illusions are deliberately constructed by researchers so as to exploit the assumptions that ordinary vision relies on.
  3. (C)Viewers who are told exactly how an illusion works go on seeing it just as strongly as they did before being told.
  4. (D)Some perceptual assumptions, such as the assumption that light comes from above, turn out to be shared by people raised in very different places and cultures.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

The explanation says the assumptions rarely mislead because they fit the settings that formed them. It predicts that people placed in settings where the assumptions are false will be misled more. If newcomers to such settings are misled no more often than natives, the fit between assumption and environment is not doing the work the author claims for it.

Why not (B): This repeats what paragraph 4 already says about illusions being 'carefully built to exploit' the assumptions, so it is consistent with the explanation rather than a challenge to it.

Why not (C): This bears on paragraph 3's point that knowledge does not alter what is seen, not on the paragraph 4 explanation of why the assumptions seldom mislead.

Why not (D): Shared assumptions fit paragraph 3's suggestion that some were formed 'over the history of the species'; the option says nothing about whether the assumptions mislead when they are false of a setting.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about illusions surviving knowledge, which is true according to the passage but belongs to a different claim.

Difficulty: Requires isolating the specific causal explanation in paragraph 4 and seeing which result breaks its prediction; two wrong options restate claims the passage already makes. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P02-001-Q4 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
In paragraph 3, the statement that 'the guess is tightly disciplined by the evidence' most nearly means that
  1. (A)repeated correction trains the brain, over time, to stop guessing and simply register the signal as it is.
  2. (B)the interpretation perception settles on is held in check by what the incoming signal actually contains.
  3. (C)perception refuses to adopt any expectation that the viewer has not personally confirmed by experience.
  4. (D)the viewer consciously tests each perceptual guess against careful measurement before accepting it as true.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The phrase answers the objection 'why do we not simply see whatever we expect?' The reply is that the evidence constrains which interpretation wins, so expectation cannot override the signal at will. The correct answer states this constraint.

Why not (A): This brings back the recording picture of paragraph 1, which the author rejects; the passage says every glance remains 'a small act of interpretation'.

Why not (C): The passage allows that some assumptions may come from 'the history of the species', and it says nothing about perception refusing unconfirmed expectations.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the conclusions are 'reached on our behalf, before anything is presented to awareness', so conscious testing is the opposite of the claim; the measuring viewer of paragraph 3 still sees the illusion.

Common misconception: Reading 'disciplined' as a conscious checking process rather than a constraint built into the inference.

Difficulty: The phrase has to be read against the objection it answers in the preceding sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-002 · Perception, attention and memory · passage Easy · 446 words · 4 questions

Most people believe that anything sufficiently striking will catch their eye. If a person in a gorilla suit walked through a game of catch we were watching, we assume we would notice. A well-known series of experiments suggests otherwise. When viewers are asked to count the passes made by one team in a short video, a large share of them fail to see an unexpected figure stroll through the middle of the scene, even when it pauses to face the camera. Asked afterwards, many insist that nothing unusual happened, and some are indignant when the recording is replayed.

The phenomenon, usually called inattentional blindness, is easy to misread as a failure of the eyes. It is not, or at least not in any sense that an optician could detect: the light from the unexpected figure reaches the retina as surely as the light from the ball. What fails is the selection that decides which parts of the scene will be built into conscious experience. Attention is not a spotlight that merely brightens what we are looking at; it is closer to a gate, and what does not pass through the gate is not dimly seen but, for practical purposes, not seen at all.

The finding matters less because of its surprise value than because of the confidence it exposes. We are poor judges of what we would notice, because the things we miss leave no trace in experience from which we could learn that we missed them. Ordinary experience, which might be expected to correct our overconfidence, cannot do so, because an occasion on which we failed to notice something is, from the inside, indistinguishable from an occasion on which there was nothing to notice. A driver who looks at a junction while thinking about something else may have no sense that a cyclist was in view. Afterwards he will report, quite sincerely, that the cyclist came from nowhere. His honesty is not in question; his access to his own lapse is.

This should not be taken to mean that attention is simply defective. A mind that admitted everything in the visual field to awareness would be overwhelmed, unable to follow the ball, read a page or hold a conversation in a crowded room. Selectivity is the price of focus, and on most days, when little that we fail to notice turns out to matter, the price is low. The lesson is narrower and more practical: where the cost of missing something is high, we should not rely on the conviction that we would have seen it, and should instead design tasks, roads and warning signals on the assumption that attention is already spoken for.

CAT-VARC-P02-002-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, viewers fail to see the unexpected figure because
  1. (A)attention lets only part of what reaches the eye into conscious experience.
  2. (B)they do notice the figure faintly but forget the impression before they are questioned about the video.
  3. (C)the figure appears too briefly and too indistinctly to register clearly on a short recording.
  4. (D)the light from an unexpected object does not reach the retina while the eyes are busy tracking a moving ball.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 locates the failure in 'the selection that decides which parts of the scene will be built into conscious experience', and compares attention to a gate. The correct answer restates this.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says what does not pass the gate 'is not dimly seen but, for practical purposes, not seen at all', which rules out a faint impression later forgotten.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says the figure strolls through the middle of the scene and 'pauses to face the camera', so it is neither brief nor indistinct.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the light 'reaches the retina as surely as the light from the ball'; the failure is not in the eyes.

Common misconception: Assuming the problem is visual (the eyes or the recording) rather than attentional.

Difficulty: The cause is stated in one sentence of paragraph 2, and each wrong option is ruled out by an explicit phrase. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-002-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The remark that the driver's 'honesty is not in question; his access to his own lapse is' implies that
  1. (A)he is probably concealing his inattention at the junction in order to avoid being blamed for what happened.
  2. (B)cyclists who appear suddenly at junctions are among the most common causes of collisions with careful drivers.
  3. (C)he glimpsed the cyclist dimly at the junction but has since lost the memory of that fleeting impression.
  4. (D)his account is sincere yet mistaken, since a cyclist he never consciously saw left nothing in his experience to report.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 says the things we miss 'leave no trace in experience from which we could learn that we missed them'. The driver therefore tells the truth as he experienced it, but his experience lacks the cyclist, so the report is wrong without being dishonest.

Why not (A): The passage says he reports 'quite sincerely' and that his honesty 'is not in question', which is the opposite of concealment.

Why not (B): The passage makes no claim about the causes or frequency of collisions; the example illustrates how a lapse can be invisible to the person who makes it.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says what fails to pass the gate of attention is 'not dimly seen but, for practical purposes, not seen at all', so there was no glimpse to lose.

Common misconception: Choosing the dim glimpse option, which sounds psychologically plausible but contradicts the gate image of paragraph 2.

Difficulty: One wrong option is ruled out only by connecting paragraph 3 to the gate claim in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-002-Q3 · Author's tone / attitude · EasyMCQ
The author's attitude towards the selectivity of attention is best described as
  1. (A)alarmed, regarding it as a serious defect of the mind that ought to be corrected through training.
  2. (B)accepting, treating it as the price of focus while warning against trusting it where misses are costly.
  3. (C)dismissive, treating the experiments as amusing curiosities with little bearing on ordinary life.
  4. (D)admiring, holding that attention can be relied on to admit whatever genuinely matters in a scene.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 calls selectivity 'the price of focus' and says the price is usually low, but advises against relying on 'the conviction that we would have seen it' where the cost of missing is high. The attitude is acceptance with a practical caution.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says the finding 'should not be taken to mean that attention is simply defective' and proposes changing tasks and signals, not training attention away.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the finding matters 'less because of its surprise value' than for what it exposes, and paragraph 4 draws practical lessons for roads and warning signals.

Why not (D): The driver example in paragraph 3 shows attention excluding something that matters a great deal, and paragraph 4 warns against relying on it where misses are costly.

Common misconception: Reading the warning about high-stakes settings as general alarm about attention.

Difficulty: The attitude is stated in the opening sentences of the final paragraph. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-002-Q4 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following design decisions best reflects the lesson drawn in the final paragraph?
  1. (A)Asking pilots how confident they are that they would spot an unexpected obstacle, and grounding those who report low confidence.
  2. (B)Training drivers to widen their attention so that they take in the whole visual field at once rather than one part of it.
  3. (C)Fitting a machine with an alarm that halts the task in progress, so that an operator is not relied on to glance at a light.
  4. (D)Placing a warning light directly in the operator's line of sight, on the grounds that anything plainly in view will be noticed.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The final paragraph says that where misses are costly we should design 'on the assumption that attention is already spoken for'. An alarm that interrupts the task does not depend on spare attention, so it fits the lesson.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says 'We are poor judges of what we would notice', so self-reported confidence is the conviction the author warns us not to rely on.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says a mind that admitted everything in the visual field 'would be overwhelmed'; the author does not propose abolishing selectivity.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 shows that a figure in the middle of the scene, facing the camera, goes unseen; being in view does not guarantee being noticed, so this rests on the very assumption the author rejects.

Common misconception: Choosing the line-of-sight option because it seems to make the warning hard to miss, forgetting that the gorilla was in plain view.

Difficulty: The tempting wrong option sounds sensible and is refuted only by applying the opening experiment. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-003 · Perception, attention and memory · passage Medium · 424 words · 4 questions

Ask people where they were when they heard of some great public shock, an assassination, a disaster broadcast live, the sudden death of a famous figure, and many will answer at once and in detail. They remember the room, who told them, what they were holding, the first thing they said. Such recollections have been called flashbulb memories, the name suggesting that a moment of intense feeling exposes the scene, as a flash exposes film, and fixes it for good. The people who hold them are rarely in doubt. Of all their memories, these seem the least likely to be wrong.

The difficulty is that when such memories have been checked, they have often turned out to be wrong in exactly the ways ordinary memories are. Researchers have questioned people within a day or two of a widely reported event and then again months or years later. The later accounts frequently differ from the earlier ones, sometimes in small particulars and sometimes wholesale: a person who first wrote that she heard the news from a colleague at work later describes, with complete assurance, watching it on television at home. Confronted with her own earlier account, she may concede that she wrote it while continuing to feel that her present memory is the true one.

What sets these memories apart, then, is not their accuracy but the confidence and vividness with which they are held; in these memories, conviction and correctness have come apart. One explanation is that the events are rehearsed endlessly. They are discussed with friends, revisited in anniversary broadcasts and retold until the story has a familiar shape. Each retelling is less a replay than a reconstruction, and the most recent reconstruction is the one that feels like the original. Emotion contributes too, but perhaps less to the record than to the sense of its importance: a moment that mattered seems as though it must have been remembered faithfully.

None of this means that strong feeling has no effect on memory. People rarely forget that the event happened, and there is reason to think that emotion sharpens memory for whatever is central to an experience while letting the periphery blur. The trouble is that the details we are proudest of recalling, the room, the messenger, the coffee cup, are precisely the peripheral ones. The practical lesson extends well beyond public events. Wherever memory is used as evidence, the vividness of a recollection and the certainty of the person reporting it are weaker guides to its truth than we are inclined to believe.

CAT-VARC-P02-003-Q1 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ
In paragraph 3, the phrase 'conviction and correctness have come apart' means that
  1. (A)how sure people feel about these memories no longer tracks how accurate the memories are.
  2. (B)people remember the shocking event correctly but feel unsure about the details surrounding it.
  3. (C)people grow steadily less confident in these memories as the memories become less accurate over the years.
  4. (D)each memory splits into a true core and an invented portion, which the person can readily tell apart.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

The sentence contrasts 'accuracy' with 'the confidence and vividness with which they are held'. Saying conviction and correctness have come apart means the two no longer go together: confidence stays high while accuracy falls.

Why not (B): This reverses the passage: people are most certain of the surrounding details, 'the room, the messenger, the coffee cup', which are the ones that drift.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says the later, changed accounts are given 'with complete assurance', so confidence does not fall as accuracy does.

Why not (D): The passage says people cannot tell: the woman feels 'that her present memory is the true one' even when shown her earlier account.

Common misconception: Assuming the two must move together, so that falling accuracy would bring falling confidence.

Difficulty: The meaning is fixed by the first half of the same sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-003-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The example of the woman who concedes that she wrote the earlier account suggests that
  1. (A)people readily give up a vivid memory once they are shown a written record that contradicts what they now recall.
  2. (B)evidence that a memory has changed need not shake the feeling that its present version is the authentic one.
  3. (C)she has knowingly revised her story over the years so as to make it more dramatic when telling it to others.
  4. (D)accounts written in the shock of the moment are less reliable than those formed after a period of calm reflection.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

She accepts the documentary evidence yet still feels her present memory is true. The example shows that recognising a discrepancy does not dislodge the sense of authenticity, which paragraph 3 then explains: 'the most recent reconstruction is the one that feels like the original'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says she concedes writing the account 'while continuing to feel that her present memory is the true one', so she does not give the memory up.

Why not (C): The passage describes her later account as given 'with complete assurance' and explains change by unwitting reconstruction; nothing suggests deliberate embellishment.

Why not (D): The passage treats the early account as the check against which later ones are found to have changed, and says later versions are reconstructions; nothing suggests the early record is the weaker one.

Common misconception: Assuming that a rational person shown the evidence would revise the memory, and so choosing the first wrong option.

Difficulty: Requires reading the concession together with what she continues to feel. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-003-Q3 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best describes the role of the final paragraph in the passage?
  1. (A)It turns to a separate subject, the use of memory as evidence in courts, for which the earlier paragraphs have not prepared the reader.
  2. (B)It withdraws the earlier claim that flashbulb memories are often inaccurate, in the light of evidence that emotion strengthens memory.
  3. (C)It grants a real effect of emotion on memory and shows that the grant leaves the main argument standing.
  4. (D)It offers readers a reliable technique for telling their accurate flashbulb memories apart from their inaccurate ones.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The paragraph opens with a concession ('None of this means that strong feeling has no effect on memory'), notes that emotion sharpens central detail, then shows that the details people are surest of are peripheral, so the concession does not rescue them. It ends by generalising the lesson.

Why not (A): Courts are never mentioned; the remark about memory 'used as evidence' generalises the passage's own conclusion rather than opening an unprepared topic.

Why not (B): The paragraph keeps the claim: it says the proudly recalled details 'are precisely the peripheral ones', which emotion lets blur, and ends by restating that vividness is a weak guide to truth.

Why not (D): No technique is offered; the paragraph explains why the most vivid details are the least trustworthy, which is a caution, not a method.

Common misconception: Taking the opening concession as a retreat from the thesis.

Difficulty: Students must see how the central and peripheral distinction turns the concession back in favour of the thesis. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-003-Q4 · Except / not supported · DifficultMCQ
Each of the following is supported by the passage EXCEPT:
  1. (A)Emotion may improve memory for the features of an experience that were central to it.
  2. (B)Repeated retelling of an event can make a reconstructed version of it feel like the original memory.
  3. (C)A person can accept that her earlier account differs from her present one without ceasing to trust the present one.
  4. (D)How vivid a memory is and how certain its owner feels give no indication at all of whether it is true.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The final sentence says vividness and certainty 'are weaker guides to its truth than we are inclined to believe'. A weaker guide is still some guide, so the claim that they give no indication at all overstates the passage. The other three statements are each found in the text.

Why not (A): Supported by paragraph 4: 'there is reason to think that emotion sharpens memory for whatever is central to an experience'. The hedge in the passage matches the hedge 'may'.

Why not (B): Supported by paragraph 3: each retelling is 'less a replay than a reconstruction, and the most recent reconstruction is the one that feels like the original'.

Why not (C): Supported by paragraph 2: the woman concedes she wrote the earlier account 'while continuing to feel that her present memory is the true one'.

Common misconception: Rejecting the hedged statement about emotion because it seems to cut against the thesis, and accepting the extreme statement because it sounds like the thesis.

Difficulty: The unsupported option is an exaggeration of the author's own conclusion, while one supported option looks like a counterpoint; telling them apart needs attention to degree. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P02-004 · Perception, attention and memory · passage Easy · 425 words · 4 questions

We tend to speak of forgetting as a failure, the mind's equivalent of a leaking roof: students blame it for poor marks, older people fear it as a sign of decline, and whole industries promise to reduce it. Behind these attitudes lies an assumption that an ideal memory would keep everything. Even our vocabulary betrays the attitude, since we speak of 'losing' a memory as though it were a possession that had slipped out of a pocket. It is worth asking whether the assumption behind such talk is true.

Consider what such a memory would be like to live with. Every time you parked your car, you would recall not only today's space but every space you had ever used, each with equal force. Every time you met a friend, you would be confronted with each version of her face across the years. The problem would not be a shortage of information but a surplus of it, because the memory you needed would be buried among the memories you did not. Some people who report unusually complete recall of their own lives describe the gift less as a pleasure than as a burden, a past that will not stay in the past.

Seen in this light, much forgetting looks less like decay than like editing. Memories that are rarely used become harder to reach, while those that are used often stay accessible, and the effect is less a loss than a reordering of priorities. This is roughly what a sensible filing system would do: it would keep the files you consult near at hand and move the rest to the back, on the reasonable bet that the past use of a memory predicts its future use. Some evidence even suggests that recalling one memory actively suppresses its competitors, so that using your new phone number makes the old one fade faster.

This argument has limits, and it is easy to push it too far, for not all forgetting is useful: the losses caused by brain injury or dementia are not editing but damage, and nothing said here makes them less painful. Nor does a system that is well designed on average serve every individual case: the name you cannot recall at an important meeting is a real cost, even if it is the price of a sensible policy. The point is only that the capacity to forget is part of what makes memory useful, not simply a flaw in it. A memory that never let go would be a worse memory, not a better one.

CAT-VARC-P02-004-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
The passage is primarily concerned with
  1. (A)describing the biological processes through which memories that are seldom used gradually decay in the brain.
  2. (B)recommending that people give up memory aids so that their minds can edit stored information more efficiently.
  3. (C)questioning the belief that an ideal memory would keep everything, by showing how forgetting helps memory work.
  4. (D)arguing that the fear of forgetting in old age is unfounded, since most memory loss turns out to be beneficial.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 names the assumption 'that an ideal memory would keep everything', paragraphs 2 and 3 show why forgetting is useful, and paragraph 4 limits the claim. The correct answer captures the target and the method.

Why not (A): The passage describes no biological processes and says forgetting looks 'less like decay than like editing'.

Why not (B): The passage makes no recommendation about memory aids; its conclusion is only that the capacity to forget is 'part of what makes memory useful'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says the losses of 'brain injury or dementia are not editing but damage', so the author does not dismiss fears of decline.

Common misconception: Overextending the thesis to all memory loss, which the final paragraph explicitly blocks.

Difficulty: The thesis is announced in paragraph 1 and restated at the end. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-004-Q2 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The example of recalling every parking space one has ever used serves to
  1. (A)show that people with very complete recall are unusually good at everyday tasks such as finding their cars.
  2. (B)demonstrate that most forgetting is caused by the brain running short of room in which to store information.
  3. (C)suggest that memories of places are forgotten more readily than memories of faces.
  4. (D)illustrate how holding every memory with equal force would bury the one that is needed.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 uses the parking spaces to make the point that 'the memory you needed would be buried among the memories you did not'. The example illustrates the cost of total retention.

Why not (A): The passage says such recall is described 'less as a pleasure than as a burden'; the example shows a cost, not a skill.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says the problem 'would not be a shortage of information but a surplus of it', which is the reverse of a storage shortage.

Why not (C): Places and faces are two parallel illustrations of the same point; the passage never compares how quickly they are forgotten.

Common misconception: Missing the point of the thought experiment and reading it as a claim about storage capacity.

Difficulty: The function is stated two sentences after the example. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-004-Q3 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following is most closely analogous to the account of forgetting given in paragraph 3?
  1. (A)A library that withdraws books from its shelves once readers report that they contain factual errors.
  2. (B)A library that sends rarely borrowed books to a distant store, keeping much-borrowed titles on the open shelves.
  3. (C)A library that shelves every book it has ever owned in the main hall, arranged strictly in order of arrival.
  4. (D)A library that destroys a share of its old books at random each year to free space for new acquisitions.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 3 compares memory to a filing system that keeps frequently consulted files near at hand and moves the rest 'to the back', betting that past use predicts future use. Moving rarely borrowed books to a store keeps them but makes them harder to reach, on the same bet.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 makes accessibility depend on 'past use', not on accuracy; withdrawing books for error is a different principle.

Why not (C): This is the keep-everything memory of paragraph 2, where nothing is moved back, which the author presents as the problem rather than the solution.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 describes memories becoming 'harder to reach' according to how often they are used; random destruction is neither selective by use nor a matter of reduced access.

Common misconception: Picking the random-destruction library because it involves losing books, which seems like forgetting, while ignoring that the forgetting in the passage is selective by use and a matter of reduced access rather than loss.

Difficulty: Two features must both match: selection by frequency of use, and reduced access rather than destruction. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-005 · Perception, attention and memory · passage Medium · 429 words · 4 questions

A familiar demonstration from the study of chess runs as follows. A position from a real game is shown for a few seconds and then removed, and players are asked to reconstruct it on an empty board. Strong players replace most of the pieces correctly, while novices manage only a handful, and the obvious conclusion is that strong players simply have better memories. The less obvious and more instructive part of the demonstration comes next. When the same pieces are scattered at random, in arrangements that could never arise in play, the masters' advantage largely disappears, and they do little better than beginners.

What the masters possess, then, is not a larger store for holding pieces but a different way of seeing them. Years of play have given them a vocabulary of familiar configurations, a castled king with its pawns in front, a bishop aimed down a long diagonal, and they take in a position as a few such units rather than as two dozen separate pieces. A novice looking at the same board sees wood on squares. The master sees a situation, and often, before any conscious calculation has begun, a sense of what the situation calls for.

Similar observations have been made in fields as different as radiology, birdwatching and the reading of geological maps, where experienced specialists frequently report knowing that something is wrong with an image within a second or two, before they can say what it is. The birdwatcher who names a distant species from the way it flies, long before any marking can be made out, is drawing on the same resource. It is tempting to describe this as intuition, as though it were a mysterious faculty standing apart from knowledge. It is better described as knowledge that has been absorbed into perception, so that it no longer needs to be consulted, because it shapes what is seen in the first place.

This view has practical consequences, though they are easily overstated. If expertise lies partly in the eye, it cannot be fully transmitted by rules and explanations; the learner has to look at a great many cases, with feedback, until the patterns become visible. But perception is not the whole of expertise. Masters still calculate, and careful specialists still check their first impressions, because a pattern that is recognised instantly can also be misrecognised instantly. The trained eye is fast and usually right within the territory that trained it, and that qualification matters: presented with a case unlike those it has learned from, its speed becomes a liability rather than an asset.

CAT-VARC-P02-006 · Perception, attention and memory · passage Medium · 436 words · 4 questions

There is a small puzzle about time that most people have noticed without ever stating it. A week spent travelling somewhere new seems to vanish while it is happening, so that there is never enough of it; yet once the traveller is home, the same week looks enormous, crowded with places and faces, as if it had lasted a month. A week at a familiar desk behaves in the opposite way. It drags while it is lived, each afternoon longer than the last, and then collapses in memory into a single grey item that seems to have taken no time at all.

The puzzle dissolves once we accept that we judge duration in two quite different ways. While an interval is passing, our sense of its length depends largely on how much attention we pay to time itself: boredom draws attention to the clock and stretches the hour, while absorption draws it away and the hour shrinks. Looking back, however, we have no clock to consult. We estimate how long a period lasted from what it left behind, and a period that left many distinct memories is judged to have been long. Novelty therefore works in opposite directions on the two judgements, shortening the interval as experienced and lengthening it as remembered.

This distinction sheds light on a complaint that becomes common in middle age, that the years go faster than they used to. The complaint is almost always retrospective. Few people find that a particular afternoon passes faster at fifty than at fifteen; what they find is that last year, viewed as a whole, seems to have been brief. If remembered duration tracks the number of distinct memories, the explanation is not far to seek, since adult life is more routine than youth, with fewer firsts, and a year of repeated weeks leaves less behind to measure it by.

The account is persuasive, but it should be held loosely. It is unlikely that a single factor explains how time seems at every age, and other changes, in health, in responsibility, in how far ahead people habitually plan, probably play a part. Still less does it follow, as some cheerful advice suggests, that one can lengthen a life simply by filling it with novelty. A crowded year may indeed look longer in retrospect, but it will feel shorter while it is lived, and there is no obvious reason to prefer one of these measures over the other. What the distinction teaches is not a technique for buying time but a caution against speaking of how long something 'really' felt, as though there were only one answer.

CAT-VARC-P02-007 · Perception, attention and memory · passage Difficult · 602 words · 5 questions

For much of its short history as a subject of research, the wandering mind was treated as noise. Participants in experiments were supposed to attend to the task in front of them, and the moments when their thoughts drifted to lunch or to an argument with a sibling were lapses to be minimised or averaged away. When researchers began to study these lapses directly, by interrupting people at random moments in their day and asking what they had been thinking about, the first surprise was how common the lapses were. The method had the merit of catching thought in the act, before the thinker had time to tidy it into a more respectable account of where her attention had been. A considerable part of waking thought, it turned out, is about something other than what the thinker is doing.

Two quite different interpretations of this finding have since competed. On the first, mind wandering is a failure of control: it is associated with errors in reading and driving, with poorer performance on tests of sustained attention and, in some studies, with lower reported mood at the moment of wandering. The mind that drifts is the mind that has lost its grip, and the remedy is training in attention. On the second interpretation, these costs are real but partial, and they ignore what the drifting mind is actually doing. Much wandering thought is directed at the future, at plans, rehearsals of conversations and the weighing of unfinished business, and some of the solutions that arrive unbidden in the shower or on a walk may owe something to the same process. On this view the mind that drifts is not idle but working on a different problem, one it has judged, so to speak, more pressing than the task at hand.

Each interpretation, I think, makes the same mistake, which is to speak of mind wandering as though it were one thing with one value. The evidence for costs comes largely from tasks that demand continuous attention, where any lapse is damaging by design, while the evidence for benefits comes largely from situations in which the external task is undemanding, so that little is lost by leaving it. Tasks of the first kind are chosen precisely because they make lapses easy to count, and situations of the second kind because they leave room for thought. It is hardly surprising that the two literatures reach different verdicts, since they are, for the most part, looking at different occasions. Nor does the link with low mood settle the matter. A person who is worried is likely both to feel worse and to find his thoughts pulled towards his worries, so that wandering may be as much a symptom of unhappiness as its cause.

A more useful question is not whether the mind should wander but when, and with what awareness. Drifting during a lecture one has chosen to attend is a cost; drifting while washing dishes may be a free opportunity to think. There also seems to be a real difference between thought that slips away unnoticed and thought that is allowed to roam, as when a person deliberately sets a problem aside and lets her attention go where it will. The second kind is under a loose but genuine control, since the person can notice it and call it back. If this is right, the goal of training attention should not be to abolish wandering, an aim that is probably unachievable and possibly harmful, but to make wandering more visible to the wanderer, so that she can tell which kind of occasion she is in.

  4. (D)Mind wandering is chiefly productive work on plans and unfinished problems, and its costs have been exaggerated by narrowly designed experiments.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says only that wandering 'may be as much a symptom of unhappiness as its cause', a point used to unsettle one argument; it is a detail, and the option hardens it into a claim the author does not make.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 redirects training rather than abandoning it, aiming 'to make wandering more visible to the wanderer', and calls abolition only 'probably unachievable and possibly harmful'.

CAT-VARC-P02-008 · Perception, attention and memory · passage Difficult · 581 words · 5 questions

In most schools a test is understood as a thermometer. It is applied at the end of a period of learning to find out how much has been learned, and like a thermometer it is supposed to leave the thing it measures unchanged. The understanding is so familiar that it seldom occurs to anyone that it might be false. Yet one of the more robust findings in the psychology of memory is that the act of retrieving something from memory is not a neutral reading: it changes the memory, and generally strengthens it, so that material which has been recalled once is more likely to be recalled again than material which has merely been seen again.

The contrast is clearest when the two activities are set against each other. Suppose two groups of students study the same passage, after which one group rereads it while the other closes the book and writes down whatever it can remember. The design holds constant both the material and the time spent on it, so that the only difference between the groups is whether they looked at the passage again or tried to bring it back unaided. Tested a few minutes later, the rereaders often do as well or better; tested a week later, the group that practised recall tends to remember considerably more. The advantage of retrieval, in other words, is easily hidden by the timing of the measurement, which may be one reason why it was overlooked for so long by teachers and students alike.

What is more striking is how little the students themselves sense any of this. Asked to predict their own performance, rereaders tend to be the more confident group. Rereading produces a feeling of fluency, in which the sentences are familiar and slide past easily, and ease of processing is readily mistaken for mastery. Attempting recall produces the opposite feeling, since it is effortful, it exposes gaps and it is often unpleasant, which is presumably why students left to themselves so rarely choose it. Teachers are not immune to the same illusion, since a class that has just reread its notes looks, and feels, better prepared than one that has just struggled to recall them. The method that feels like learning and the method that produces it are, at least here, not the same, and the lesson generalises uncomfortably: a learner's own sense of how well a session went is an unreliable guide to how much it achieved.

It would be easy to draw from all this a crude conclusion, that schools should simply test more, but the finding does not support it. What strengthens memory is the effort of retrieval, not the apparatus of examination, and the apparatus can work against the effort. A high-stakes test that provokes anxiety, or that is followed by no feedback, may teach students little except to fear tests, and a wrong answer recalled confidently and never corrected may itself be strengthened. The useful practice is humbler than the word 'test' suggests: it consists of frequent, low-stakes attempts to recall, each followed by a chance to check, built into ordinary study. None of this requires abolishing examinations, which serve purposes of certification that have little to do with memory; it requires only that they not be mistaken for the whole of what testing can do. Understood in this way, the thermometer turns out to be one of the more effective means of raising the temperature it was meant only to record.

CAT-VARC-P02-009 · Judgement and decision-making · passage Easy · 431 words · 4 questions

Experts who manage risk often speak of the public with weary patience. People, they observe, fear the wrong things. They worry about a nuclear plant that has never harmed anyone in the district while driving, without a second thought, along a road that kills several of their neighbours every year. They demand inquiries after a single dramatic accident and shrug at the steady toll of falls in the home. On this account the task of policy is to correct a problem of perception: to replace vivid fears with sober numbers, so that resources follow expected deaths rather than headlines.

There is something to this, but less than its confidence suggests. When people are asked to describe what makes a hazard frightening, their answers are not random. Risks feel worse when they are imposed rather than chosen, when they could kill many people at once rather than a few at a time, when their effects are delayed or invisible, and when those who create them are not those who bear them. None of these features is captured by a count of expected fatalities, yet none of them is obviously foolish. A person who accepts the dangers of rock climbing but objects to a factory releasing an untested chemical into the river is not confused about arithmetic. She is drawing a distinction between a risk she has consented to and one that has been placed on her by others, and that distinction is a moral one that most legal systems also recognise.

Seen this way, the gap between expert and lay rankings of hazards is partly a disagreement about what the question is. The expert asks how many people will die; the citizen asks, in addition, whether the arrangement that produces those deaths is fair, controllable and worthy of trust. To call the second question irrational is to settle a dispute about values by redescribing it as a dispute about facts.

None of this makes dread a reliable guide. The features that make a hazard feel threatening can be manufactured by vivid reporting, and fear can drive choices that increase the very danger it is meant to avoid. If travellers abandon aircraft for cars after a well-publicised crash, they may exchange a small risk for a larger one without noticing the trade. The sensible response is neither to dismiss public fear as noise nor to treat it as a verdict. It is to take it apart: to ask which of its components reflect values that deserve a hearing in policy, and which reflect only the salience of a recent and alarming image.

  3. (C)argue that public fear of some hazards partly expresses defensible values, while warning that such fear still needs to be examined.
  4. (D)show that expert rankings of hazards are mistaken and that policy should defer to the rankings the public prefers.

Paragraphs 2 and 3 argue that features such as consent, catastrophe and fairness make dread partly a matter of values, not arithmetic; paragraph 4 adds that 'None of this makes dread a reliable guide' and that fear must be taken apart. The correct answer holds both halves.

Why not (A): Reporting appears only in paragraph 4 as one source of distortion; the passage is chiefly about the values dread can express, so this is too narrow.

Why not (B): This is the experts' view reported in paragraph 1, which paragraph 2 says has 'less than its confidence suggests'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says public fear should not be treated 'as a verdict', so deferring to lay rankings reverses the author's position.

Common misconception: Choosing the experts' view because it opens the passage, or the reporting point because it is vivid.

Difficulty: The thesis and its qualification are both stated outright, and each wrong option is clearly one-sided or narrow. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-010 · Judgement and decision-making · passage Easy · 424 words · 4 questions

Few findings from the psychology of decision-making have travelled as far as the idea that too much choice is bad for us. The claim has a satisfying shape. It flatters the suspicion that modern abundance has not made people happier, and it offers a tidy remedy: fewer options, simpler shelves, shorter menus. Retailers have trimmed product lines in its name, and essayists have used it to diagnose a whole culture of anxious shoppers frozen before walls of nearly identical goods. Its appeal, in short, owes at least as much to its moral resonance as to the strength of the evidence on which it was originally built.

The evidence, however, is less tidy than the slogan. A handful of striking early experiments suggested that people offered many options were less likely to choose at all, and less satisfied when they did. Later attempts to reproduce such effects produced a far more mixed picture: sometimes the effect appeared, sometimes it vanished, and sometimes larger assortments made people more likely to buy. A finding that reverses under slightly different conditions is not a law of human nature. It is a clue that the conditions themselves are doing the work.

What might those conditions be? The more careful research points to a few. Large sets seem to burden people when they lack settled preferences in the domain, when the options differ along many dimensions that are hard to compare, when no option is marked as a sensible default, and when the decision feels consequential enough to invite later regret. A wine enthusiast choosing among forty bottles is browsing; a novice choosing among forty pension funds is drowning. The number of options is the same. What differs is whether the chooser has a way of sorting them, and therefore whether the abundance in front of him registers as an opportunity or as an accumulation of possible mistakes for which he alone will be answerable.

This reframing matters because it changes the remedy. If abundance itself were the problem, the answer would be to cut. If the problem is the absence of structure, the answer is often to organise: to group options by the features people care about, to supply defaults that can be overridden, to let people narrow the field in stages. Cutting still has its place, particularly when most of the options are poor or nearly identical. But a policy of reduction applied everywhere would deprive the enthusiast of what she values in order to rescue the novice from a difficulty that better design could have removed.

  3. (C)showed that larger assortments always made people more likely to buy, so that the early findings were simply reversed.
  4. (D)found the effect in some cases, found nothing in others, and sometimes found that larger assortments increased buying.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 calls the picture 'far more mixed' and says the effect sometimes vanished or reversed, which is not consistent confirmation.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says larger assortments 'sometimes' increased buying; the option turns one of three reported outcomes into an invariable rule.

Common misconception: Overreading the mixed results as a simple reversal or as a verdict on the early work.

CAT-VARC-P02-011 · Judgement and decision-making · passage Medium · 419 words · 4 questions

Ask people to estimate a quantity they do not know, after first asking whether it is higher or lower than some number, and their estimates will drift toward that number. The effect survives when participants have watched the number being produced at random, when they are told it is meaningless, and when they are paid for accuracy. It appears among amateurs and professionals alike, in guesses about the age of a building, the price of a house or a reasonable figure with which to open a negotiation. That arbitrary numbers can move consequential judgements is now one of the least disputed findings in the study of decision-making. Why they do so is more disputed, and the answer matters more than it first appears.

The older explanation treats the anchor as a starting point. People begin at the number they are given and adjust away from it toward what seems plausible, but they stop too soon, at the first value that falls within a range they can accept. On this view anchoring is a failure of effort: the mind is a reluctant traveller who halts at the nearest acceptable station. The remedy follows naturally. Slow people down, ask them to think harder, give them reasons to care, and they will travel further from the anchor.

A second explanation locates the problem earlier. When we are asked whether a building is older than, say, two hundred years, we do not merely note the number; we test it, and testing a hypothesis tends to mean searching for evidence that would make it true. We recall the building's worn stone and its old-fashioned windows, and these recollections, now freshly available, shape the estimate that follows. The anchor does not set a starting point so much as select the evidence. If this account is right, effort is no cure, because a more diligent search conducted in the same direction only gathers more of the same evidence.

The two accounts are not exclusive. When people produce an anchor themselves, as when they start from a figure they already know and correct it for some difference, something like insufficient adjustment seems to occur, and in those cases encouraging effort does help. But for anchors supplied by others, which are the ones that negotiators, sellers and advocates supply deliberately, the evidence favours the selective account, and with it a different remedy: not to think harder about the number one has been handed but to think deliberately against it, by asking what would make it wrong.

  3. (C)participants have been told the correct answer to the question before giving their estimate.
  4. (D)the anchor is mentioned only after participants have already written down their estimates.
Answer and explanation

Why not (C): The passage never describes participants who know the answer; the effect concerns quantities people 'do not know'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 describes anchors given 'first', before the estimate; nothing is said about anchors that come afterwards.

Common misconception: Guessing from general knowledge instead of the list in paragraph 1.

Difficulty: A direct paraphrase of one clause in paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-011-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ

  1. (A)give the same estimate but feel less confident in it, because the extra search would raise doubts about the anchor.
  2. (B)disregard the anchor entirely, because longer reflection would reveal that the number was arbitrary and irrelevant.
  3. (C)stay close to the anchor, because the extra search would mostly turn up more evidence consistent with it.
  4. (D)move further from the anchor, because the extra time would let adjustment continue beyond its usual stopping point.

Answer: (C)

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says the effect survives even when people are told the number is 'meaningless', so knowing it is arbitrary is not enough.

Why not (D): This is the prediction of the older account in paragraph 2; paragraph 3 says that on the selective view 'effort is no cure'.

Common misconception: Applying the older account's remedy to the newer account.

Difficulty: Both accounts are described in neighbouring paragraphs and make opposite predictions about effort. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-012 · Judgement and decision-making · passage Medium · 445 words · 4 questions

It is often said that overconfidence is the most pervasive of the biases, the one from which many of the others draw their strength. The claim is repeated so often that it is easy to miss how much it conceals. 'Overconfidence' names at least three different things, and they do not always travel together. Treating them as one has made the phenomenon seem both more universal and more mysterious than it is.

The first is overestimation: believing that one has performed, or will perform, better than one actually does. The second is overplacement: believing that one is better than other people. The third, which receives less public attention, is overprecision: excessive certainty that one's beliefs are accurate, shown when people asked for a range they are ninety per cent sure contains some true value give ranges that contain it far less often than that. These sound like variations on a single vanity, but they come apart in revealing ways. On tasks that are easy, people tend to underestimate how well they have done, even as they place themselves above others; on tasks that are very hard, the pattern reverses, and many people will rate themselves below average at, say, juggling or composing verse while still overestimating their own scores.

What explains such reversals is less a desire to think well of oneself than the way information is distributed. People usually know more about their own performance than about other people's, so their judgements of others are pulled toward a vague middle. When a task is hard, they sense that they did poorly, if not quite how poorly, but assume that others did less badly; when it is easy, the opposite. A good deal of what looks like ego, in other words, is a predictable consequence of reasoning from imperfect information about the people with whom one is being compared.

Overprecision is different. It shows no comparable tendency to reverse, appearing on easy and hard questions, among novices and among specialists, and it is the form that does most harm in planning, where projects are budgeted against ranges too narrow to include what actually happens. It is also the form that most resists exhortation: telling forecasters to be humble rarely widens their intervals much. More promising are procedures that change the question, such as asking people to assign probabilities to several ranges in turn, so that the possibility of an outcome far from their best guess must be considered explicitly rather than waved away. The lesson is not that confidence is good or bad but that a single word has been doing the work of three, and that each of the three needs its own diagnosis.

CAT-VARC-P02-013 · Judgement and decision-making · passage Medium · 418 words · 4 questions

If biases are errors, it seems natural to suppose that teaching people about them should reduce them. Hence the many workshops in which managers, doctors and analysts are taken through a catalogue of the mind's failings, from anchoring to the sunk-cost fallacy, in the hope that knowing the names will help them avoid the traps. The hope is reasonable, and it is mostly disappointed. Participants usually leave such sessions able to define the biases and to spot them in descriptions of other people's reasoning. What they far less often do is reason differently when a fresh problem, dressed in unfamiliar clothes, arrives in their own work.

Part of the difficulty is that biases rarely announce themselves. A textbook problem about sunk costs is labelled, at least implicitly, by the chapter in which it appears; a real decision about whether to keep funding a struggling project comes with no such label, and with colleagues, budgets and reputations attached. Recognising a pattern in a tidy example is a different skill from noticing it in a messy situation, and teaching the first does not automatically produce the second. There is also a subtler obstacle. People readily accept that biases exist and readily see them at work in others, while judging their own conclusions to be the products of careful thought. Instruction can even sharpen this asymmetry, supplying a vocabulary for diagnosing colleagues without unsettling one's own sense of seeing clearly.

None of this shows that debiasing is hopeless. A few interventions built around repeated practice, varied examples and immediate feedback on one's own errors have produced improvements that outlasted the session, though how far they reach beyond the kinds of problem used in training remains uncertain. And there is a quieter strategy that sidesteps the question of individual reform altogether: changing the procedures within which decisions are made. A committee that collects independent estimates before discussion, or a review that requires someone to argue the case against a favoured plan, does not need its members to have become wiser. It builds the correction into the routine.

The contrast is instructive. Approaches that rely on informing the individual treat bias as a kind of ignorance, curable by knowledge. Approaches that redesign procedures treat it as a standing feature of minds working under ordinary conditions, to be managed rather than eliminated. The second view is less flattering, but it fits the evidence better, and it has the practical merit of not depending on each person's ability to catch themselves in the act.

CAT-VARC-P02-014 · Judgement and decision-making · passage Medium · 455 words · 4 questions

Debates about intuition tend to fall into two camps that talk past each other. One camp collects cases in which experienced professionals, asked to explain a snap judgement, cannot say how they knew, and yet were right: the nurse who senses that a patient is deteriorating before the monitors register anything, the engineer who distrusts a design at a glance. The other camp collects cases in which confident experts did no better than simple formulas, or than chance, at predicting outcomes in their own fields. Each camp treats its own cases as the typical ones and the other camp's as curiosities.

A way through the impasse begins by asking what intuition is. On the most plausible account it is recognition: the rapid matching of a present situation to patterns stored from earlier ones, which brings with it a sense of what is likely to happen next and of what to do. Recognition of this kind can be only as good as the patterns it has learned, and patterns can be learned only where two conditions hold. The environment must contain regularities that link cues to outcomes, and the person must have had extensive practice with feedback quick and clear enough to reveal whether a judgement was right. A ward where patients either recover or worsen within hours satisfies both conditions. A market in which prices reflect the guesses of many other participants, and in which the consequences of a choice emerge slowly and mixed with luck, satisfies neither very well.

This account explains why both camps can find their cases. It also yields a less comfortable conclusion. The feeling of knowing that accompanies intuition is produced in the same way whether or not the underlying patterns are valid. A forecaster who has spent years in an environment with little regularity can feel as sure as a nurse, because confidence grows with familiarity and with the coherence of the story one can tell, not with accuracy. Subjective certainty therefore tells us almost nothing about whether to trust a judgement. What tells us something is the environment in which the judgement was learned.

The practical upshot is not that intuition should be confined to a list of approved professions. The same expert may work in a high-validity environment for some tasks and a low-validity one for others. A clinician may become very good at judging, within minutes, whether a patient needs urgent attention, while long-range predictions about the same patient's recovery, whose outcomes arrive years later and are shaped by many intervening events, remain little better than guesses. The question to ask of any intuition, then, is not who holds it or how firmly, but whether the conditions for learning it were ever present.

CAT-VARC-P02-015 · Judgement and decision-making · passage Difficult · 585 words · 5 questions

The demonstration is by now familiar. Two groups are offered the same treatment, described in two ways: to one group as leaving ninety in a hundred patients alive after a year, to the other as seeing ten in a hundred die. The numbers are identical, yet the first description reliably attracts more support than the second. Since the treatment does not differ, the argument runs, a rational agent should not respond to the difference in wording, and the fact that people do respond is evidence that their preferences are shaped by presentation rather than substance. The principle appealed to, that logically equivalent descriptions should produce the same choice, has seemed so obvious that few have thought it needed defending.

Yet the principle is less obvious than it looks once we remember that descriptions do not fall from the sky. Somebody chooses them, and the choice is rarely arbitrary. A doctor who believes a treatment is worth trying tends, without any intent to manipulate, to speak of the patients it saves; a doctor with doubts more naturally mentions those it fails. Speakers also tend to describe a quantity in terms of the reference point from which it has moved: a glass that was full and has been partly drunk is more often called half empty than half full. If such regularities hold, then the frame carries information that the bare numbers do not, and a listener who draws an inference from it is not being swayed by mere wording. She is reading what the speaker's choice of words reveals, much as she would read an emphasis or a pause.

On this view, many framing effects are better described as sensitivity to implicit information than as failures of logic, and the experimenter who insists on equivalence has mistaken the logic of sentences for the logic of conversation. The equivalence holds between the propositions; it does not hold between the acts of uttering them, because those acts tell the hearer different things about the speaker. Participants who bring their ordinary conversational competence into the laboratory are, in a sense, being too reasonable for the experiment's purposes.

This rehabilitation should not be pushed too far. When experiments go to some lengths to make the frame visibly arbitrary, for example by assigning descriptions by an evident coin toss or by presenting both descriptions together, the inference from frame to speaker has nothing to work on, and a rational participant should set it aside. Effects in such conditions are often smaller, which is what the information account predicts, but they do not always vanish. Some residue seems to reflect the plain pull of words such as 'die' and 'lose', a reaction that would survive any amount of reflection about speakers. The information account explains a good deal of the phenomenon; it does not explain all of it.

What follows is a more discriminating verdict than either side usually offers. Whether responsiveness to a frame is a lapse depends on whether, in the situation at hand, the frame was a signal. Outside the laboratory it usually is, which is why habits formed in conversation are worth having. The danger lies precisely in that usefulness. A listener who reasonably treats frames as informative can be exploited by a speaker who knows the convention and chooses a frame to suggest a recommendation he would not dare to state outright. The fault in such cases lies less with the listener's inference than with the speaker's abuse of a norm that ordinarily makes the inference sound.

  4. (D)descriptions take on their meaning only once the listener already knows the numbers that they are describing.

CAT-VARC-P02-016 · Judgement and decision-making · passage Difficult · 585 words · 5 questions

For several decades one research tradition in psychology has proceeded by a simple method. Set people a problem whose correct answer is fixed by logic or probability theory, record the ways in which their answers depart from it, and infer from the pattern of departures the shortcuts the mind must be using. The method has been extraordinarily productive. It has yielded a catalogue of systematic errors, and with it a picture of human judgement as a set of serviceable but crude rules of thumb that fail predictably whenever a problem is constructed to expose them. That picture has travelled well beyond psychology, into economics, law and medicine, where it is now invoked to justify the design of forms, defaults and warnings meant to protect people from their own shortcuts.

A rival tradition objects not to the findings but to the frame. A rule of thumb, it argues, can be judged only against the environment in which it is used, and the problems of the laboratory are often unlike the environments in which human judgement developed and in which it is mostly exercised. More provocatively, it points out that simple rules can outperform elaborate ones. When a decision must be made from a small sample in a noisy world, a model that weighs many cues carefully will fit the data at hand closely, including its accidents, and will then predict new cases worse than a rule that attends to one or two strong cues and ignores the rest. Ignoring information, which the first tradition tends to read as a symptom of limited capacity, can on this account be a source of accuracy.

The point is sound, and it is worth seeing why it does not abandon the idea of a norm. The advantage of the simple rule is itself a result of statistical theory: it accepts a little systematic error in exchange for a large reduction in sensitivity to noise, and in small, noisy samples that exchange is often a good one. To praise frugal heuristics on these grounds is not to reject normative standards but to replace one standard, agreement with a probabilistic calculation on a fully specified problem, with another, accuracy in predicting cases one has not yet seen. That second standard is arguably the one that matters for creatures who must act on incomplete information.

Where the rival tradition overreaches is in moving from what heuristics can do to what people actually do. To show that a rule performs well in some environments is not to show that people use it in those environments and abandon it in others. That further claim, that minds select their tools to suit the structure of the task, is an empirical hypothesis, and the evidence for it is uneven. People sometimes apply a rule where it serves them and sometimes carry it into settings where it misleads, and modern life supplies many settings, from the interpretation of medical test results to the assessment of financial risk, that resemble the laboratory more than the forest or the village market.

The disagreement, then, is narrower than its polemics suggest. Both traditions accept that judgement relies on shortcuts, and both, when pressed, accept that a shortcut can be good or bad depending on where it is used. What separates them is a question that neither has fully answered: how well people match their methods to their circumstances. Answering it requires studying environments as carefully as minds, which neither tradition, in its more confident moments, has been much inclined to do.

  3. (C)people's own reports, given after the task, of the rules they believe they followed.
  4. (D)studies of the environments in which human judgement first developed and is now exercised.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1: 'record the ways in which their answers depart from it, and infer from the pattern of departures the shortcuts the mind must be using.'

Why not (A): Prediction of new cases is the rival tradition's concern in paragraph 2, not the method of the first tradition.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 describes no self-reports; the tradition infers shortcuts from errors on problems with fixed answers.

Why not (D): Attention to environments is what the rival tradition demands, and paragraph 5 says neither tradition has studied them carefully.

Common misconception: Attributing the rival tradition's interests to the first tradition.

Difficulty: The method is set out in one sentence of paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-017 · Emotion, motivation and wellbeing · passage Easy · 461 words · 4 questions

Boredom is usually described as a kind of emptiness, the feeling of having nothing to feel. On this view it is a residue, what remains when interest drains away, and the natural response is to fill the space with whatever is at hand, much as one might fill an awkward silence with remarks about the weather. The description is understandable, since boredom rarely announces any object of its own, but it gets the state almost exactly backwards. Boredom is not an absence. It is an active and often insistent signal that the activity in front of us is failing to engage the capacities we have available, and it exists to make us do something about that.

The comparison with other unpleasant states is instructive. Hunger is disagreeable, but nobody concludes from this that hunger is a defect; its discomfort is the means by which it gets us to eat. Boredom works in a similar way. A person bored by a repetitive clerical task does not merely register that the task is dull; she feels restless, glances at the clock, begins to imagine alternatives. The unpleasantness is doing work. It pushes attention away from a situation that offers too little and towards a search for one that offers more, whether that means a harder problem, a different companion or simply a walk.

None of this implies that boredom is always benign. A signal is useful only when there is some way to act on it, and many situations offer none. The patient in a waiting room, the passenger on a delayed train and the worker who cannot leave a monotonous post all receive the message without being able to respond, and in such cases boredom curdles into frustration or something close to despair. There are also signs that people will sometimes reach for almost any stimulation rather than endure an unoccupied mind, even stimulation they would otherwise find unpleasant. The signal, in other words, can be loud enough to drive choices that serve no one, and a theory of boredom that celebrated it without reservation would be as misleading as one that dismissed it.

What follows is a reason to be wary of the most popular remedy. If boredom is a message about the fit between a person and an activity, then an endless supply of small distractions does not answer the message; it merely muffles it. Someone who reaches for a screen at every lull may never feel bored for long, but may also never learn what the boredom was pointing at: a job that has stopped stretching them, a routine that has outlived its purpose, a curiosity that has gone unfed. The more useful question, when boredom arrives, is not how to make it stop but what it is about.
