CAT-VARC-P02-018 · Emotion, motivation and wellbeing · passage Easy · 487 words · 4 questions

Popular advice about habits tends to treat them as a contest of will. Bad habits persist, on this account, because people lack the resolve to stop, and good ones fail to form because people give up too soon. The remedy is accordingly motivational: set firmer goals, remind yourself why they matter, and try harder. This view has the appeal of moral simplicity, since it locates every failure in a lack of character and every success in effort, and it is repeated in a great deal of well-meant advice. The trouble with this picture is that it misdescribes what a habit is. A habit is not a desire that has grown strong but a response that has become attached to a context, so that the context itself, rather than any present intention, sets the behaviour going.

Consider someone who checks her phone each time she sits down on the morning train. At first the action may have been chosen, perhaps as a way of passing an otherwise idle part of the trip, but after hundreds of repetitions it no longer needs choosing. The seat, the motion of the carriage and the familiar stretch of the journey are enough to bring the hand to the pocket, and she may find the phone in her hand before she has formed any wish to look at it. Asking her to resolve more firmly misses the point, since resolve is not what produces the act. The cue is.

If this is right, the most effective way to change a habit is often to change the circumstances that trigger it, not to strengthen the will that opposes it. Putting the phone in a bag rather than a pocket, sitting in a different carriage, or giving the first minutes of the journey to some specific task all interrupt the link between cue and response. Each of these requires a single decision rather than a renewed act of resistance every morning, which is precisely why such changes tend to outlast good intentions. It is often observed that people find it easier to alter their routines after moving house or changing jobs, and the account explains why: the old cues have simply disappeared, and with them much of the pressure to repeat old behaviour.

Two cautions are in order, however, if the argument is not to be pushed further than it can go. First, not every repeated behaviour is a habit in this sense; some acts are repeated because the person goes on wanting the result each time, and for these, motivation does matter. Second, the power of context cuts both ways. The same disruption that frees someone from an unwanted routine can dissolve a welcome one, which is why a regular exercise habit so often lapses after a move. Willpower is not irrelevant, then, but it is a poor tool for a job that the environment, suitably arranged, performs more cheaply and more reliably.

  3. (C)she lacks the firmness of resolve that would allow her to keep the phone in her pocket.
  4. (D)she has a strong wish to look at it each time she sits down in the carriage.

Common misconception: Accepting the original choice as the present cause, though the passage says the act no longer needs choosing.

Why not (D): This supports the role of goals and motivation, which is the popular picture the author argues against, so it does not strengthen the account.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about moving because it mentions a change of place, without checking that it bears on the claim.

Difficulty: The correct answer must be matched to the precise causal claim; the wrong options are on topic but test nothing about cues versus intentions. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-019 · Emotion, motivation and wellbeing · passage Medium · 425 words · 4 questions

Few findings in happiness research have travelled further than the idea of hedonic adaptation. People who experience a good or bad change in their circumstances, the finding goes, tend over time to drift back towards their earlier level of wellbeing, as though each of us were tethered to a personal set point from which events can pull us only temporarily. In its popular form the idea has hardened into something like a philosophy: since we adapt to everything, the pursuit of better circumstances is futile, and contentment is a matter of temperament rather than of how one lives. The research, read with any care, gives considerably less support to this conclusion than its popularity would suggest.

To begin with, adaptation is uneven. People appear to adjust quite quickly to many gains, such as a higher salary or a larger house, which soon become the new normal against which further comparisons are made. But some conditions resist adaptation stubbornly. Persistent noise, a long and unpredictable commute and chronic pain seem to remain unpleasant year after year, perhaps because they keep demanding attention and never settle into the background of experience. The lesson is not that circumstances do not matter but that they matter selectively: what we stop noticing stops affecting us, and what keeps intruding does not.

A second problem lies in how the evidence is summarised. Many studies report that, on average, a group's wellbeing returns towards its earlier level some time after an event such as marriage or the loss of a job. An average, however, can conceal very different individual paths. A group in which some people end up much happier and others much less happy can show almost no change overall, and the reassuring flat line then describes almost nobody in it. When researchers have examined individuals rather than averages, they have tended to find that a substantial minority do not return to where they began, which is precisely the information that a headline about set points discards.

None of this means that the original insight was mistaken. People do adapt to a great deal, and the observation usefully corrects the belief that one more purchase or promotion will produce lasting delight. The error lies in the step from 'we adapt to much' to 'nothing we do makes a lasting difference'. That step is no more justified than its opposite, and it carries a practical cost, since it can discourage people from changing the very conditions, the noisy flat or the gruelling journey, that are most likely to keep wearing them down.

Why not (B): Severity is never compared; paragraph 2 explains the resistance by the fact that such conditions 'keep demanding attention'.

Why not (C): Comparison with earlier states is mentioned only for gains that become 'the new normal'; it is not offered as the reason noise resists adaptation.

Common misconception: Supplying a plausible real-world reason (severity, suddenness) in place of the one the passage gives.

Difficulty: The reason is stated in a single sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-020 · Emotion, motivation and wellbeing · passage Medium · 450 words · 4 questions

One influential account of curiosity holds that it arises from a gap between what a person already knows and what that person would like to know. On this view curiosity is not a general appetite for novelty but a response to a specific, noticed absence: the missing end of a story, the answer to a riddle, the identity of the person who has just telephoned. The account has much to recommend it. It explains why we are rarely curious about subjects of which we know nothing, since we cannot notice a gap in a field we cannot see, and why a little knowledge is often more stimulating than none. It also explains why curiosity so frequently vanishes the moment the answer arrives, leaving behind a satisfaction that is oddly short-lived.

Yet the information gap account fits some cases far better than others. It is at its strongest when the gap is well defined, as in a puzzle with a single solution or a mystery with a culprit. It is weaker when applied to the kind of curiosity that drifts without a target, the impulse that leads someone to wander along the shelves of a library, to turn over stones on a beach or to follow a footnote simply to see where it leads. In these cases there is no specific question waiting to be answered. If anything, the person seeks out situations in which questions might arise, and the pleasure lies as much in the searching as in any particular discovery.

Defenders of the account sometimes reply that the wanderer is simply responding to many small gaps in quick succession. The reply preserves the theory, but at a cost. If any movement of attention whatever can be redescribed after the fact as the closing of a gap, the account no longer explains anything in particular; it becomes a vocabulary rather than a hypothesis. It seems more accurate to say that there are at least two kinds of curiosity, one that seeks to close a specific gap and one that seeks to encounter the unfamiliar, and that the influential account is a good theory of the first.

The distinction matters for anyone who wishes to cultivate curiosity, and for teachers above all. A lesson built on the first kind poses a sharp question and withholds the answer just long enough to make it wanted. A lesson built on the second provides a rich environment and the time to wander in it, without specifying in advance what ought to be found. Both have their place, but a classroom organised entirely around the first may produce students who are excellent at finishing puzzles and rather less inclined to go looking for them.

  4. (D)they cannot notice what is missing in a field they are unable to see at all.

Why not (B): Difficulty and effort are never mentioned; paragraph 1 explains the point by the impossibility of noticing a gap.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says the account treats curiosity as 'not a general appetite for novelty', so this reverses the account.

Common misconception: Choosing the novelty option because 'appetite for novelty' appears in the paragraph, without noting that the account denies it.

Difficulty: The answer is a direct paraphrase of one clause in paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-021 · Emotion, motivation and wellbeing · passage Medium · 427 words · 4 questions

Ask people what grief involves and many will describe a sequence: denial, then anger, then bargaining, then depression, and at last acceptance. The sequence has become so familiar that it can seem like a discovery about human nature, as settled as the stages of a fever. It began, however, as a description of how some terminally ill patients responded to the news of their own approaching death, drawn from clinical conversations rather than from any systematic study of the bereaved. Its migration from the dying to the grieving, and from a set of observations to something closer to a timetable, happened largely outside the kind of research that might have tested it.

When grief has been studied directly, the tidy sequence has not fared well. Bereaved people commonly report several of the named feelings at once, or in no particular order, and many report some of them hardly at all. Perhaps the most striking finding is how many people cope with a loss far better than the model would lead one to expect, experiencing intense sorrow that gradually eases over months without passing through anything resembling distinct stages. Resilience of this kind is not denial in disguise; for a good number of people it seems simply to be the ordinary course that grief takes.

The harm of the model lies less in its inaccuracy than in what people do with it. A description of what some people feel becomes a standard of what everyone ought to feel, and departures from the standard begin to look like problems requiring attention. The widow who is not angry may be told that she has not yet faced her loss; the son who feels relief after a parent's long illness may wonder what is wrong with him. Friends, and even professionals, may wait for a stage that never arrives, or press a grieving person to move on to the next one as though grief were a course with a syllabus.

It would be unfair, though, to treat the model's popularity as mere credulity. It arrived at a time when dying and grief were seldom spoken of openly, and it gave people words for feelings, anger above all, that they might otherwise have hidden or been ashamed of. That was a real service, and it should not be forgotten in the rush to correct the record. The task now is to keep what the model offered, the permission to feel a wide range of things, while dropping the suggestion that those feelings must arrive in a fixed order, or arrive at all.

Common misconception: Taking the reference to clinical conversations as a claim that the model is accurate for dying patients.

CAT-VARC-P02-022 · Emotion, motivation and wellbeing · passage Medium · 404 words · 4 questions

It has become a commonplace that rewarding people for doing what they already enjoy can make them enjoy it less. Picture a child who draws for the pleasure of it and is then promised a prize for each picture. While the prizes continue, the drawing may increase. When they stop, the child may draw less than before the prizes began, as though the activity had been converted from play into work and then left unpaid. The phenomenon is real, and it has been reproduced often enough to deserve to be taken seriously. What is less often noticed is how many conditions it depends upon, and how quickly it fades when they are absent.

The effect appears most reliably when the reward is tangible, expected in advance and given for doing the task rather than for doing it well. A prize announced beforehand and handed over for every picture invites the child to understand the drawing as a means to the prize. An unexpected treat, by contrast, cannot have been the reason for drawing, and praise that tells the child something about the quality of the work may even strengthen interest, since it feeds the sense of competence that made the activity enjoyable in the first place. The same physical reward can therefore have opposite effects depending on what it communicates to the person who receives it.

Equally important, the effect presupposes that there was interest to undermine. Many tasks that people are paid or rewarded for, from sorting files to practising scales, hold little intrinsic appeal at the outset, and for these, rewards cannot crowd out an enjoyment that was never there. Indeed, a reward may keep someone at a dull task long enough for skill to grow and for the task itself to become absorbing, so that interest arrives because of the incentive rather than in spite of it.

The sensible conclusion lies between two slogans. Those who say that rewards destroy motivation generalise from a narrow set of conditions; those who say that people simply do whatever they are paid to do ignore the conditions entirely. The better question in any particular case is how the reward is likely to be read: as an attempt to control behaviour that would otherwise not occur, or as information about how well something is being done. The first reading tends to erode interest in activities that people already value; the second tends to support it.

  3. (C)comes unexpectedly and so cannot have been the child's reason for drawing.
  4. (D)is less tangible than a prize and so is harder for the child to treat as a payment.
Answer and explanation

Paragraph 2 says such praise 'may even strengthen interest, since it feeds the sense of competence that made the activity enjoyable in the first place'.

Why not (B): Nothing in the passage says praise removes any expectation; the stated reason concerns the sense of competence.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 gives this reason for the unexpected treat, not for praise about quality; the option transfers one explanation to the other case.

Why not (D): Tangibility is mentioned as a condition of the undermining effect, but the passage does not explain the benefit of praise this way.

Common misconception: Confusing the reason given for the unexpected treat with the reason given for informative praise, as both appear in one sentence.

Difficulty: The answer is in one clause of paragraph 2, next to a tempting neighbouring clause. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-022-Q4 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
In paragraph 1, the phrase 'converted from play into work and then left unpaid' suggests that the child

The phrase describes an activity that has been turned into paid work and then stripped of its pay; paragraph 2 explains that an expected prize 'invites the child to understand the drawing as a means to the prize'. Once the prize ends, the means has no point.

CAT-VARC-P02-023 · Emotion, motivation and wellbeing · passage Difficult · 609 words · 5 questions

The word 'stress' entered the study of living things from the study of materials, where its meaning is reasonably exact: the internal force per unit area within a body subjected to a load. In engineering, stress is distinguished from strain, the deformation that the load produces, and both are distinguished from the load itself. When the word was borrowed to describe what happens to organisms under pressure, these distinctions did not travel with it. In ordinary speech, and in a good deal of research, 'stress' can refer to the demanding situation, to the bodily and emotional reaction it provokes, or to the relation between the two, and it is often far from clear which of these is meant. A survey announcing that most workers feel stressed may be measuring their workloads, their symptoms or their sense of being overwhelmed, and the headline rarely says which.

The slippage is not merely a matter of untidy usage. Consider the familiar claim that stress is bad for health. If stress means the reaction, and the reaction is defined partly by its harmful effects, the claim is close to true by definition. If stress means the demanding situation, the claim is plainly false as a generalisation, since many people meet heavy demands without harm and some appear to thrive on them. Only if stress means a particular relation, demands that exceed what a person believes they can manage, does the claim become a substantive hypothesis that could turn out to be wrong. Much popular discussion moves among these meanings without noticing, and so acquires the authority of the first reading and the scope of the second.

The same confusion shadows the more recent enthusiasm for 'good stress'. To say that some stress is beneficial may mean that some demanding situations are worth seeking out, which is uncontroversial; or that some physiological reactions to demand improve performance, which is plausible within limits; or that people who interpret their reactions as helpful fare better than those who do not, which is a specific claim requiring specific evidence. Advice built on the phrase often slides from the first meaning, which everyone accepts, to the third, which is the one actually being sold. Each of the three deserves to be judged on its own evidence, and the plausibility of the first lends nothing to the third.

It might seem that the remedy is to abandon the word altogether. That would be a mistake, and not only because ordinary language is not so easily reformed. The very breadth of the term has allowed researchers in physiology, psychology, sociology and medicine to recognise that they were studying related things, and it has drawn attention to connections, between working conditions and heart disease, say, that narrower vocabularies might have kept apart. Whole programmes of research, on bereavement, on unemployment, on the burdens of caring for a sick relative, found a common frame in it. A concept can be scientifically fruitful precisely because it is loose at the edges, so long as those who use it know where the looseness lies.

What the word cannot do is carry an argument by itself. The useful discipline is to ask, whenever a claim about stress is made, which of its meanings is in play, and whether the evidence offered supports the claim under that meaning or only under a different one. A finding about reactions does not automatically license advice about situations, and a finding about interpretations says little about either. Keeping the senses apart costs a few extra words; failing to keep them apart has cost a good deal of confusion, some of it in the very places where clarity matters most.

CAT-VARC-P02-024 · Emotion, motivation and wellbeing · passage Difficult · 612 words · 5 questions

For a time it seemed that psychology had found the mechanism of willpower. Self-control, the theory held, draws on a limited resource that is used up by effort and replenished by rest. Resisting a tempting food, suppressing an emotion or forcing oneself through a tedious task would each deplete the same store, leaving a person less able to exercise control on whatever came next. The idea had the great advantage of matching experience. Most of us know the evening collapse that follows a day of holding ourselves together, and the theory seemed to explain it with all the economy of a fuel gauge. It also seemed to account for a scattering of everyday puzzles: why a dieter might lapse late in the day rather than at breakfast, why hard decisions get put off until morning, why a quarrel flares more easily after a draining afternoon.

The theory has fared badly under closer scrutiny. Large coordinated attempts to reproduce the basic effect, in which many laboratories ran the same procedure, found it to be far smaller than early reports had suggested and in some cases close to nothing. A particularly vivid version of the theory, which identified the resource with blood sugar and held that a sweet drink could restore flagging self-control, met an additional difficulty: effortful thinking appears to change the brain's overall energy consumption only slightly, far too little for the proposed mechanism to work as described. Some studies also reported that people who believed willpower to be plentiful showed little or no depletion, a result awkward for any theory in which the resource is a physical quantity.

Yet it does not follow that the experience the theory set out to explain is an illusion. People do grow less willing to persist after sustained effort, and that change deserves an account. A more promising one treats the feeling of mental fatigue not as a reading on an emptying tank but as a signal about priorities. On this view, continued effort on one task carries an opportunity cost, the value of everything else one might be doing instead, and the sense of weariness rises as that cost accumulates, nudging attention away from obligation and towards rest, pleasure or novelty. What looks like running out is better described as a shift in what the mind is willing to pay for.

The difference between the two pictures is not merely academic. If willpower is a fuel, the sensible strategy is rationing: avoid unnecessary decisions, save strength for what matters, and accept failure when the tank is low. If fatigue is a signal about value, the strategy changes. Tasks that feel meaningful or rewarding should be less draining than equally demanding tasks that do not, and changing how a task is framed, or what it leads to, may restore persistence where rest alone would not. The second picture does not make self-control easy, but it relocates the problem from the size of a store to the weighing of competing goods. It also suggests why the same evening can feel exhausting at a desk and effortless at a hobby, though the demands on attention may be much the same.

The episode also carries a lesson about how attractive ideas spread. The resource model was simple, intuitive and flattering to common experience, and these virtues helped it travel far beyond the evidence that supported it. None of this required anyone to be careless or dishonest; it is simply how persuasive ideas tend to behave. Its decline is not a refutation of everyday wisdom about tiredness; it is a reminder that a vivid metaphor can capture a real phenomenon while getting its mechanism entirely wrong.

CAT-VARC-P02-025 · Development and learning · passage Easy · 455 words · 4 questions

Adults watching a three-year-old serve imaginary tea to a row of stuffed animals tend to see one of two things. Some see a charming waste of time, a pause in the serious business of growing up that will be outgrown once the child can read and count; others see a rehearsal of adult roles, a small person practising to be a host. Both readings miss what is most remarkable about the scene, which is that to pour tea that is not there, the child must hold two descriptions of the world in mind at once: the cup is empty, and in the game the cup is full. She must keep the game's version from leaking into the real one, so that she does not actually try to drink, and yet follow its consequences faithfully, so that a toppled cup leaves the bear 'wet'.

This double bookkeeping is the same machinery that underlies counterfactual thought, the ability to ask what would happen if things were otherwise. A child who reasons that the bear is wet because the tea spilled is tracking a causal chain inside a supposition. Planning, explanation and much of science rest on exactly this skill, since to decide whether a plan will work, or why a bridge failed, one must build a world that differs from the actual one in a single respect and then trace what follows. Seen in this light, pretend play looks less like a holiday from thinking than like one of its earliest training grounds.

Some caution is nevertheless in order. That play and counterfactual reasoning draw on a shared capacity does not show that more play produces better reasoners; it may be that children who are already good at holding alternatives in mind simply enjoy pretending more. Nor does every game of make-believe involve careful causal tracking; much of it is repetitive and loose. The claim worth defending is narrower: pretence gives young children a space in which suppositions can be entertained without cost, and in which the rules that govern them can be tried and broken without anything real being at stake.

That narrower claim has a practical edge. Well-meaning efforts to make early childhood more productive often convert free play into guided activities with stated learning outcomes. Some guidance helps, and an adult who joins a game and asks what happens next can enrich it. But a game that exists to deliver a lesson changes character, because the child who knows that a right answer is wanted stops supposing freely and starts guessing what the adult has in mind. What made pretence valuable, its freedom from consequence, is the very thing that the attempt to harness it is most likely to destroy.

  3. (C)Treat the cup as full inside the game while knowing it is empty, and let events in the game have their fitting effects.
  4. (D)Believe for the length of the game that the cup really holds tea, so that the pretence feels convincing to her.

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 says the child must 'hold two descriptions of the world in mind at once' (the cup is empty; in the game it is full), keep the game's version separate from reality, and 'follow its consequences faithfully'. The correct answer states both requirements.

Why not (A): In paragraph 1 a toppled cup is part of following the game's consequences 'faithfully', leaving the bear 'wet'; a spill breaks no rule.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 reports the 'rehearsal of adult roles' reading only to say that it misses 'what is most remarkable about the scene'; it is not what the child must do.

Why not (D): This reverses paragraph 1, which says she must 'keep the game's version from leaking into the real one', which is why she does not try to drink.

Common misconception: Choosing the 'rehearsal of adult roles' option because it is mentioned in the passage, without noticing that the author rejects it as incomplete.

Difficulty: The answer is set out in the last three sentences of paragraph 1; the wrong options are each contradicted by a nearby phrase. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-026 · Development and learning · passage Easy · 435 words · 4 questions

Few ideas about teaching have travelled as far, or met as little resistance, as the notion that each learner has a style. Some children, it is said, are visual learners, others auditory, others kinaesthetic, and each will learn best when lessons are delivered in the matching form. The idea is attractive for honest reasons: it seems to respect individual difference, it gives teachers something concrete to do with that respect, and it fits the everyday experience that people do prefer some ways of taking in information to others.

The trouble lies in the step from preference to performance. That people report preferences is not in dispute. What the theory needs is something stronger: that a learner labelled visual will learn more from a visual lesson than from a spoken one, while a learner labelled auditory will show the reverse. Testing this requires sorting learners by style, teaching each group in both ways, and checking whether the advantage flips from one group to the other. When the claim has been tested in this demanding way, the predicted crossover has generally failed to appear. Learners tend, on the whole, to benefit from the same methods whatever label they carry, and whatever preference they would have reported if asked.

What does seem to matter is the fit between the form of a lesson and the content being taught. A coastline is better learned from a map than from a description, whoever the learner is; the sound of a language is better learned by listening; a knot is learned by tying it. Here the mode is chosen for the material, not for the student. The same holds in reverse: a pupil who insists that she learns best by listening will not master the shape of a coastline sooner because it is described to her aloud, however vivid the description. A teacher who presents geography through maps is not catering to visual learners, but simply using the form in which geographical knowledge is most naturally held.

None of this means that variety in the classroom is pointless. Moving between pictures, speech and activity can hold attention and can give a learner several routes back to the same idea. The objection is to the diagnosis rather than to the variety. Labelling a child as one kind of learner invites both the child and the teacher to treat the other channels as closed, and a child told that she is 'not a reading person' may come to believe it. The cost of the theory is not that it wastes a few lessons, but that it narrows expectations while appearing to widen them.

CAT-VARC-P02-026-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
Which of the following best expresses the main point of the passage?
  1. (A)The best form for a lesson depends on its subject matter, which is why maps are the natural way to teach geography.
  2. (B)The belief that matching lessons to a learner's style improves learning lacks support, and labelling learners by style can do harm.
  3. (C)Teachers should present every lesson in visual, spoken and active forms so that learners of every style are reached.
  4. (D)People do not really have preferences about how they take in information, so the idea of learning styles is a myth.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 argues that the predicted crossover 'has generally failed to appear', and paragraph 4 concludes that labelling 'narrows expectations while appearing to widen them'. The correct answer captures both the lack of support and the harm.

Why not (A): This is the point of paragraph 3 alone; it leaves out the central critique of the matching claim in paragraph 2 and the cost of labelling in paragraph 4.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 defends variety for other reasons (attention, several routes back to an idea); delivering lessons so that every style is reached keeps the diagnosis the author rejects.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 states that 'That people report preferences is not in dispute'; the author denies only that preferences predict performance.

Common misconception: Picking the option about matching form to content, which is true to one paragraph but too narrow for the whole.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated directly; the wrong options are a contradiction, a prescription and a single-paragraph point. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-026-Q2 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to paragraph 2, which pattern of results is the learning styles theory committed to predicting?
  1. (A)The advantage of one teaching method over another reverses depending on which style a group of learners has been assigned.
  2. (B)Learners who state a clear preference for one form of instruction learn faster than learners who state none at all.
  3. (C)Learners classed as visual outperform learners classed as auditory whatever form the lesson is delivered in.
  4. (D)Learners of every style do better when a lesson combines visual, spoken and practical elements in a single session.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says the theory needs a visual learner to learn more from a visual lesson 'while a learner labelled auditory will show the reverse', and that testing it means 'checking whether the advantage flips from one group to the other'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 compares groups taught in both ways; it says nothing about learners with no preference learning more slowly.

Why not (C): A group that wins in every format shows no flip; paragraph 2 says the theory needs the visual group to do better with visual lessons and the auditory group with spoken ones.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the theory predicts that the better method 'flips' between groups; a uniform benefit from mixed lessons is not a matching prediction.

Common misconception: Confusing the claim that visual learners simply do better with the claim that the better method differs by group.

Difficulty: The prediction is spelled out in two adjacent sentences of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 names three 'honest reasons' for the idea's appeal, matching the other three options. Paragraph 2 says that when tested in the demanding way 'the predicted crossover has generally failed to appear', so the correct answer is not a reason given; it is contradicted.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 says it 'fits the everyday experience that people do prefer some ways of taking in information to others'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says the idea 'gives teachers something concrete to do with that respect'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 lists this first: 'it seems to respect individual difference'.

Common misconception: Rushing past paragraph 2 and assuming the idea's popularity reflects confirming evidence.

CAT-VARC-P02-026-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ
Which of the following findings, if true, would most weaken the argument of paragraph 2?
  1. (A)Teachers who accept the theory of learning styles report feeling noticeably more confident when they plan lessons for mixed classes.
  2. (B)Students taught a stretch of coastline from maps remembered it better than students given only a spoken description of it.
  3. (C)Most learners, when asked, can readily name a way of receiving information that they prefer over the others available.
  4. (D)Learners sorted by style and taught in both formats showed a reversal: visual learners gained more from diagrams, auditory learners more from talk.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 rests on the claim that, under a proper test, 'the predicted crossover has generally failed to appear'. A properly designed test that found the crossover would undercut exactly that claim.

Why not (A): Teachers' confidence says nothing about whether matched teaching improves learning, which is the claim at issue in paragraph 2.

Why not (B): This supports paragraph 3, where form is matched to content 'whoever the learner is'; it does not show any crossover between kinds of learner.

Why not (C): The author already grants this in paragraph 2 ('That people report preferences is not in dispute'), so it leaves the argument about performance untouched.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about preferences because it sounds like support for learning styles, without noticing that the author concedes it.

CAT-VARC-P02-027 · Development and learning · passage Medium · 441 words · 4 questions

Students and teachers share a reasonable-sounding rule: if learning feels smooth, it is going well. A chapter reread the night before an examination seems familiar, a set of problems practised one type at a time feels increasingly easy, and the growing ease is taken as proof of mastery. The rule is reasonable because in many activities fluency really does track competence. A pianist who plays a passage without stumbling has, in some sense, learned it. Yet in the specific matter of building knowledge that lasts and transfers, the rule is frequently misleading, and sometimes backwards.

The reason is that the conditions which make performance easy during study are often not the conditions which make memory durable. Rereading produces familiarity, the sense of having seen something before, which is easily mistaken for the ability to recall it unaided. Practising one kind of problem in a block lets the learner apply the same procedure again and again without ever deciding which procedure the problem calls for, the very decision a mixed examination demands. By contrast, spacing study sessions apart, testing oneself instead of rereading, and mixing problem types all make study feel slower and more error-prone, and all tend to produce better retention weeks later. The effort of retrieving a half-forgotten fact, or of working out which method applies, appears to be part of what fixes it in memory.

It would be easy to draw the wrong lesson from this, namely that harder is always better. It is not. A difficulty helps only when the learner can, with effort, overcome it; material pitched far beyond a student's grasp produces confusion rather than consolidation, and a test on something never learned teaches little except discouragement. The useful difficulties are those that call on processes the learner will later need, such as retrieval, discrimination and reconstruction, rather than obstacles that merely slow things down. Printing a textbook in a faint, awkward typeface makes reading harder, but there is little reason to expect it to make anything stick.

The deeper problem is one of judgement. Because learners rely on how study feels, they systematically prefer the methods that serve them worst, and they are not foolish to do so: the costs of effortful methods are felt now, while their benefits arrive later and unannounced. Teachers face a similar temptation, since a lesson in which students struggle looks, to an observer and to the students themselves, like a lesson that has gone badly. Changing practice therefore requires more than information about which methods work. It requires a willingness to distrust a feeling that is usually reliable, in exactly the setting where it is not.

CAT-VARC-P02-027-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, practising one type of problem at a time falls short because it
  1. (A)spares the learner the decision about which procedure a problem needs, a decision a mixed examination requires.
  2. (B)produces more errors during study than mixed practice does, and the errors discourage learners from continuing.

  4. (D)takes much longer than rereading the chapter, and so leaves the student too little time for review.

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says block practice lets the learner apply one procedure 'without ever deciding which procedure the problem calls for, the very decision a mixed examination demands'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says the reverse: it is mixing problem types that makes study 'more error-prone', while blocked practice feels easy.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says block practice lets the learner apply 'the same procedure again and again', which builds fluency rather than preventing it.

Why not (D): The passage never compares the time taken by blocked practice and rereading; both are described as methods that feel easy.

Common misconception: Reversing which method is error-prone during study.

Difficulty: The answer is a close paraphrase of one sentence in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-027-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author would most likely regard a quiz given to students on a topic they have never been taught as
  1. (A)useful chiefly because it shows students how much of the topic still remains for them to learn later.
  2. (B)an unhelpful difficulty, since there is nothing learned for the effort of retrieval to consolidate.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 rejects the lesson 'that harder is always better' and says a difficulty helps 'only when the learner can, with effort, overcome it'.

CAT-VARC-P02-027-Q3 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ
The author's attitude toward learners who prefer rereading and blocked practice is best described as
  1. (A)sympathetic to the reasons for their choice while holding that the choice is mistaken.
  2. (B)approving, since the fluency these methods produce is usually a reliable sign of real competence.

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4 says learners 'systematically prefer the methods that serve them worst' yet 'are not foolish to do so', because costs are felt now and benefits arrive later. The tone is understanding but critical.

Why not (B): The author grants that fluency usually tracks competence (paragraph 1) but says that for durable learning the rule is 'frequently misleading', so the methods are not endorsed.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says 'they are not foolish to do so', and adds that information alone is not enough to change practice, so scorn is the opposite of the author's tone.

CAT-VARC-P02-027-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following practices best fits the passage's account of a useful difficulty?
  1. (A)A student copies out key passages with her non-dominant hand during revision, so that each line takes longer to finish.
  2. (B)A student attempts problems from next year's syllabus before learning this year's methods, so as to struggle as much as possible.
  3. (C)A student works through problems grouped by type, raising the difficulty steadily within each group as her confidence grows over the weeks.
  4. (D)A student revising for a mixed examination shuffles problems from last month's topics, so that each must first be identified before it is solved.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 says useful difficulties can be overcome with effort and 'call on processes the learner will later need, such as retrieval, discrimination and reconstruction'. Shuffling familiar problems demands discrimination (paragraph 2: deciding which procedure applies), and last month's material is within reach.

Why not (A): This only slows things down, like the 'faint, awkward typeface' in paragraph 3; it calls on no process the student will later need.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says material 'pitched far beyond a student's grasp produces confusion rather than consolidation'; struggle for its own sake is not the point.

Why not (C): This remains blocked practice: it adds difficulty but never makes the student decide 'which procedure the problem calls for' (paragraph 2).

Common misconception: Treating any added difficulty as useful, especially the graded blocked practice, which feels demanding but lacks the discrimination step.

CAT-VARC-P02-028 · Development and learning · passage Medium · 475 words · 4 questions

A long tradition explains exceptional performance by talent: the chess master and the concert violinist are thought to have been born with something the rest of us lack. A body of research on practice has done much to unsettle this view. Those who reach the top of skilled fields, it turns out, have typically spent very large amounts of time not merely doing the activity but practising it in a particular way: working on specific weaknesses, at the edge of their current ability, with prompt feedback, usually under the eye of a teacher. Experience alone, on this account, is not enough. A person who has driven a car for twenty years has had a great deal of experience but has probably not improved since the first few, because ordinary driving involves no deliberate effort to get better.

The strongest version of this view holds that practice of the right kind is not just necessary for expertise but very nearly sufficient, so that differences in accumulated practice explain most of the differences between the great and the merely good. Popularised as a rule that a fixed number of hours will make anyone an expert, it has an obvious democratic appeal. It is also, in that form, hard to sustain. Among people who practise a great deal, the amount of practice leaves much of the variation in performance unexplained, and the size of that unexplained remainder seems to differ across domains. Where the rules are stable and feedback is immediate, as in many games and sports, practice accounts for more; where outcomes are delayed and noisy, as in forecasting markets or judging which patient will recover, it accounts for less.

This last pattern is revealing, because it suggests that what practice builds depends on what the environment can teach. Deliberate practice requires knowing, quickly and reliably, whether an attempt succeeded. A violinist hears a wrong note at once; a chess player can check a line against a stronger opponent. A financial analyst may wait years to learn whether a judgement was sound, and even then the outcome will reflect luck as much as skill. In such fields long careers can produce confidence without accuracy, and the hours a person has logged may say more about seniority than about expertise.

None of this restores the old picture of talent as a fixed endowment that settles everything. The research on practice was right that expertise is built rather than found, and right that the manner of practice matters more than its sheer quantity. What it cannot do is deliver the promise sometimes made in its name, that anyone who puts in the hours will arrive at the same place. Starting points differ, the rate at which practice pays off differs, and some fields offer so little reliable feedback that practice in the demanding sense is barely possible within them.

  3. (C)the excessive faith that researchers have placed in counting hours of practice as a measure of skill.

Paragraph 3 says that in fields where feedback is slow and noisy 'long careers can produce confidence without accuracy': years of work make people sure of themselves without making their judgements correct.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says the violinist 'hears a wrong note at once'; the violinist is the example of good feedback, not of misplaced confidence.

Why not (C): The phrase describes practitioners in fields with poor feedback, not researchers; the critique of hour counting comes in paragraph 2 and paragraph 4.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 attributes this state to 'long careers', not to beginners who have practised too little.

Common misconception: Attaching the phrase to the wrong group (beginners, researchers) instead of reading the sentence it sits in.

Difficulty: The surrounding sentence defines who has the confidence and why it is unearned. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

  1. (A)a fair summary of what the research on practice has shown about how experts are made.

CAT-VARC-P02-028-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ

  2. (B)Top chess players began playing at an earlier age, on average, than the many chess players who never managed to reach the top level of the game.

CAT-VARC-P02-029 · Development and learning · passage Medium · 450 words · 4 questions

The familiar explanation of teenage recklessness is ignorance: adolescents take risks because they do not understand the dangers, or because they believe, in some half-formed way, that harm happens only to other people. If that were the whole story, the remedy would be straightforward. Teach young people the facts about speeding, drugs and unprotected sex, and their behaviour should improve. Schools and governments have spent heavily on precisely this remedy, and its results have often been modest.

One reason is that the premise is weaker than it looks. When adolescents are asked, in a calm setting, to estimate the dangers of various activities, their answers are often not very different from those of adults, and by the middle teens many reason about risk in hypothetical cases about as well as their parents do. What distinguishes them is less what they know than what happens to that knowledge in particular situations. Decisions taken alone and at leisure look quite mature; decisions taken in the company of friends, under excitement, look very different. In laboratory games that reward bold choices, adolescents have been found to take noticeably more chances when they know their friends are watching, while the presence of an audience makes much less difference to adults.

A plausible account of this pattern appeals to the uneven timing of development. The systems that make rewards, and especially social rewards, feel intense appear to become more responsive around puberty, while the capacities for restraint and planning continue to mature into the early twenties. For several years, then, the accelerator is more sensitive than the brake, and the gap shows most clearly when peers raise the value of a daring act. It should be said that accounts of this kind are easily overstated. Brains do not determine behaviour on their own, adolescents differ widely, and plenty of teenagers who are surrounded by friends behave with conspicuous care.

If the account is broadly right, it has an uncomfortable implication for policy: the most effective interventions may be those that change the situations adolescents find themselves in rather than the beliefs they carry into them. Rules that limit the number of young passengers a new driver may carry, or arrangements that make the safe choice the socially easy one, work with the grain of adolescent decision-making rather than against it. The account also suggests a more generous reading of adolescence itself. A heightened sensitivity to peers is not simply a defect awaiting repair; it is part of what drives young people to leave the family, form their own alliances and try what their elders would not. The same disposition that makes a reckless dare attractive makes new friendships and new ideas attractive too.

CAT-VARC-P02-029-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ

  1. (A)improve noticeably once they have received formal instruction about the risks involved in them.

Common misconception: Picking the ignorance view because it is stated in the passage, without noticing that the author disputes it.

CAT-VARC-P02-029-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The passage suggests that an important reason information campaigns about risk have had limited success is that they
  1. (A)have usually given adolescents information about risks that is inaccurate or deliberately exaggerated.
  2. (B)come from schools and governments, sources whose advice adolescents are inclined to distrust and ignore.

CAT-VARC-P02-029-Q3 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following measures is most in keeping with the approach the author favours in paragraph 4?
  1. (A)A course trains teenagers to calculate the statistical risks of common activities, so that they can weigh dangers as carefully as adults do.
  2. (B)A programme asks each teenager to sign a private pledge never to ride with a reckless driver, so that the promise guides later choices.
  3. (C)A campaign shows teenagers vivid images of injuries caused by careless driving, so that the dangers of speeding are kept firmly in their minds.
  4. (D)A school arranges for evening events to end with a supervised bus home, so that riding with a newly licensed classmate becomes the awkward option.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 4 favours interventions that 'change the situations adolescents find themselves in' and that 'make the safe choice the socially easy one'. A supervised bus changes the situation and makes the safe choice the easy one in front of peers.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says adolescents already reason about risk 'about as well as their parents do'; the course adds knowledge rather than changing situations.

Why not (B): A pledge made alone works on decisions 'taken alone and at leisure', which paragraph 2 says already 'look quite mature'; it leaves the social situation unchanged.

Why not (C): This tries to change 'the beliefs they carry into' situations, the information-based approach the passage says has had modest results.

Common misconception: Choosing the pledge, which seems to address peer pressure, though it works only on calm, solitary decisions.

Difficulty: The pledge wrong option is close; the solver must apply the distinction between calm and social decisions from paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-030 · Development and learning · passage Medium · 464 words · 4 questions

It is widely believed that there is a window for learning languages, open in early childhood and closed by adolescence, after which a new language can be studied but never truly acquired. The belief has some foundation. On average, people who begin a second language in early childhood end up closer to native-like command than people who begin as adults, and the difference is clearest in pronunciation: a late learner who has spoken a language fluently for decades will often still carry an accent that a child immigrant lost within a few years. What the evidence supports, however, is a slope rather than a window. The image of a door that slams shut at a fixed age is the part of the popular picture least able to survive scrutiny.

Several things complicate the story that age alone is at work. Children and adults who move to a new country do not learn under the same conditions. The child is enrolled in school, surrounded for hours each day by speakers of the new language and given every incentive to sound like them; the adult often works among speakers of her first language, uses the new one for limited purposes and has an identity already settled in the old. When late learners are compared with early ones, these differences in the quantity and character of exposure travel along with age, and it is hard to tell how much of the outcome each explains. Where adults are immersed in the way children are, some reach levels that the stricter versions of the window theory say should be out of reach.

It also matters what is being measured. Accent appears most sensitive to the age of first exposure, and some aspects of grammar, such as fine judgements about word endings, seem sensitive too. Vocabulary, by contrast, goes on growing throughout life in a first language and a second alike, and adults often learn the early stages of a new language faster than young children do, because they bring better memory strategies and a developed grasp of how languages work. A single critical period for language as a whole therefore looks less plausible than a set of different sensitivities, declining at different rates for different components.

None of this makes early exposure unimportant, and a parent who can give a child two languages from the start gives something valuable. But the window image has costs of its own. It persuades adults that serious learning is no longer open to them, so that they invest less and expect little, and then treats the resulting modest achievement as confirmation. A more accurate picture, in which age makes some things harder and a few things much harder without closing the door at any fixed point, is less dramatic but more useful.

CAT-VARC-P02-030-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The passage is primarily concerned with
  1. (A)explaining why adults learn the early stages of a new language faster than young children usually manage to do.
  2. (B)replacing the image of a language window that shuts at a fixed age with a picture of gradual age effects that differ by component.
  3. (C)arguing that adult immigrants fall short in a new language chiefly because they are not immersed in it as children are.
  4. (D)showing that age has no real effect on how well a second language is eventually learned, once exposure is held equal.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 states the thesis ('a slope rather than a window'), paragraph 2 adds the confound of exposure, paragraph 3 the differences between components, and paragraph 4 the cost of the window image. The correct answer covers both the slope and the component point.

Why not (A): This is one point within paragraph 3, used to support the case against a single critical period; it is too narrow to be the passage's concern.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says it is 'hard to tell how much of the outcome' exposure and age each explain, so naming exposure as the chief cause overstates the author's view.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 grants that the belief 'has some foundation' and that early learners end up closer to native-like command; the author denies only the fixed window.

Common misconception: Taking the confound in paragraph 2 as the author's whole explanation, or overstating the critique into a denial of any age effect.

Difficulty: Two wrong options overstate the author's view in different directions; one is a single supporting detail. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-031 · Development and learning · passage Difficult · 594 words · 5 questions

For much of the twentieth century, average raw scores on intelligence tests rose steadily in country after country, so steadily that the tests had to be renormed at intervals to keep the average at one hundred. The rise, which came to be called the Flynn effect after the researcher who did most to document it, presents an awkward dilemma. If the tests measure intelligence and the gains are real, then our grandparents, scored against today's norms, would have been of strikingly low intelligence, and a substantial share of them would have fallen in a range we now associate with serious intellectual difficulty. That conclusion is absurd on its face, and it is worth saying why: those generations ran farms, businesses and governments, and nobody who knew them found them obtuse. If instead the gains are an artefact of testing, it is hard to see why the artefact should be so large, so persistent and so widespread.

The way out begins with a closer look at where the gains occurred. They were not spread evenly across the tests. They were largest on tasks that ask for abstract classification and for the detection of patterns in unfamiliar material, and much smaller on tasks that draw on stored knowledge, such as vocabulary and arithmetic. Consider a person asked what a spade and a pair of shears have in common. She can answer that both are tools, or that one is used to dig the flower bed and the other to trim the hedge around it. The first answer earns credit; the second does not. Yet the second is not stupid. It reflects a habit of thinking about things in terms of their practical relations, a habit well suited to a life in which what matters about a spade is whether it will break the ground before the rain comes.

On this reading, the twentieth century did not make people more intelligent in some general sense; it trained a particular style of thought. Mass schooling, work that deals in symbols rather than objects, and a culture saturated with categories and hypotheticals rewarded the habit of setting aside what things are for and attending to what kind of thing they are. The tests, built by people who had that habit, measured it well. Nor is the spread of the habit a trivial change, since it is precisely the habit that a world of written rules, formal classifications and hypothetical planning keeps demanding of the people who live in it. The gains are therefore real, since the habit genuinely spread, but they are not gains in a single underlying power that would make the average person of today a match for the most gifted minds of a century ago.

This resolution is attractive, but it has a price that is not always acknowledged. If scores on the most abstract tests reflect habits cultivated by a particular kind of society, then the tests cannot be quite what they were often taken to be, a culture-free measure of raw capacity. They remain good predictors of success in schools and workplaces, but partly because those institutions demand the very habits the tests detect. It does not follow that the tests are worthless, or that differences in scores within a generation mean nothing; among people educated in broadly similar ways, scores can still capture real differences in how well they reason. What does follow is that comparisons across generations, and across societies with very different patterns of schooling and work, need to be interpreted with far more caution than a single number invites.

  4. (D)largest on the tasks that draw most heavily on stored knowledge, such as vocabulary and arithmetic.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says scores rose 'in country after country'; no comparison of early and late schooling countries is made.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says the gains 'were not spread evenly across the tests'.

Why not (D): This reverses paragraph 2, which says gains were 'much smaller on tasks that draw on stored knowledge'.

Common misconception: Reversing which tasks showed the larger gains.

Difficulty: Located in two consecutive sentences of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-032 · Development and learning · passage Difficult · 578 words · 5 questions

The picture of the ageing mind that most people carry is one of steady decline. Memory falters, thinking slows, and the sharpness of youth gives way, from some point in early adulthood, to a long and gentle slide. The picture is not invented. On tasks that require rapid processing of unfamiliar information, such as spotting a target among distractors or holding several new items in mind while manipulating them, performance does fall with age, and the fall can be detected well before what anyone would call old age. But the picture has been assembled in a way that makes the decline look both steeper and more uniform than it is.

Much of the early evidence came from comparing people of different ages at a single moment, a method that is quick and cheap and was therefore used widely. A group of seventy-year-olds tested alongside a group of twenty-five-year-olds differs from it in more than age: the older group grew up with less schooling, different work and less familiarity with the kind of test being given. Studies that follow the same people over decades tend to show later and gentler declines than such snapshots suggest, though they carry biases of their own, since those who stay in a long study are often healthier and more able than those who drop out. Neither method gives a clean view, and the honest summary is that some decline is real, while its timing and size are less certain than the familiar picture implies.

More important, the familiar picture attends to only half of what changes. Knowledge of words, of facts, of how things usually go and how people usually behave, goes on accumulating well into later life, and on tests that draw on it older adults often outperform younger ones. Some researchers have gone further, proposing that part of the measured slowing reflects not failing machinery but a larger store: a mind that knows more words takes longer to find any one of them, much as a large library takes longer to search than a small one. The proposal is contested, and it cannot explain away every loss. It does, however, show that the same observation, a slower response, can be read as a symptom of deterioration or as a cost of accumulation, depending on what one assumes about the mind producing it.

It would be a mistake to replace the narrative of decline with an equally simple narrative of wisdom, in which age brings a serenity and judgement that compensate for everything else. Older people are not uniformly wise, and losses in speed and working memory have real consequences, for learning a new technology, for instance, or for following a fast conversation in a noisy room. The more defensible view is that ageing changes the mix of a person's capacities rather than lowering a single level, and that whether the change amounts to a loss depends on the task. In a setting that rewards speed with novel material, the young will usually have the advantage; in one that rewards the recognition of familiar patterns, a wide vocabulary or an accurate sense of how situations tend to unfold, the advantage may lie the other way. A good deal of everyday competence, from running a household to reading the mood of a tense meeting, belongs to the second kind. A society that judges older minds only by the first kind of task will find decline wherever it looks.

  3. (C)Mental decline with age is largely an illusion produced by flawed comparisons between people from different generations.
  4. (D)Ageing alters the balance among mental capacities, so whether it counts as decline depends on what a task demands.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 4 states the author's view: 'ageing changes the mix of a person's capacities rather than lowering a single level, and ... whether the change amounts to a loss depends on the task'. Paragraphs 1 to 3 build toward this by qualifying the decline picture.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 calls this 'an equally simple narrative of wisdom' and says 'Older people are not uniformly wise', so it is a view the author rejects.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says 'Neither method gives a clean view'; and the point about methods is only one step toward the central claim in paragraph 4.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 concludes that 'some decline is real', and paragraph 4 says losses in speed 'have real consequences'; the author denies only the steep, uniform picture.

Common misconception: Taking the critique of the decline picture for a denial of decline, or the rejected wisdom narrative for the author's view.

Difficulty: Each wrong option captures a position the passage discusses; the correct answer requires the author's own final position. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Difficulty: The correct answer is an attractive opposite of the criticised view; spotting it needs paragraph 4's rejection of the wisdom narrative. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P02-033 · Social psychology · passage Easy · 473 words · 4 questions

The line-judgement experiments of the 1950s are among the most retold stories in psychology, and the moral usually drawn from them is a gloomy one. A participant sits among strangers who, one after another, give an obviously wrong answer about which of several lines matches a standard; when the participant's turn comes, he often repeats their error. The lesson, we are told, is that ordinary people will deny the evidence of their own eyes rather than stand apart from a crowd. This reading has become so familiar that it is easy to forget how much interpretation it smuggles in.

Begin with the numbers that the gloomy version tends to leave out. On the critical trials, most of the answers participants gave were correct, and a sizeable share of people never yielded at all. A study that is remembered as a demonstration of conformity could as easily be remembered as a demonstration of independence under strange and uncomfortable conditions. Which of these stories gets told says more about the teller than about the data.

More important is the question of what yielding meant. Outside the laboratory, when several people who seem competent and sincere all report the same thing, the rational response is to give their reports considerable weight, even against one's own impression. Our senses err, and other people are among the best instruments we have for catching their errors. A participant who reasoned, 'I cannot see why they are all saying this, but it is unlikely that every one of them is mistaken,' was not abandoning reason; he was applying a rule that serves well almost everywhere except in a room deliberately arranged to defeat it. On this view much of what looks like spineless deference is better described as informational influence: treating others as evidence.

None of this means that the pressure in the room was imaginary. Variations in which participants wrote their answers privately, rather than announcing them aloud, produced far less agreement with the majority, which suggests that some yielding was about avoiding embarrassment rather than about belief. Participants interviewed afterwards also described acute discomfort, and some admitted that they had answered against what they saw. Normative pressure, the wish to fit in, was real. The point is not that it was absent but that it was only part of the picture, and that collapsing everything into it produces a caricature.

The caricature matters because it shapes the remedies we prescribe. If people conform because they are weak, the cure is to exhort them to be brave. If they conform partly because they are sensibly using others as sources of information, the cure is better information: making dissent visible, so that a single honest voice breaks the appearance of unanimity, and designing institutions in which the reasons behind a majority view can be inspected rather than merely counted.

  2. (B)argue that participants in the experiments yielded almost entirely because they feared embarrassment, and that their reasoning about the majority played no real part.
  3. (C)recommend that institutions train their members to trust their own perceptions and to resist the views of a majority whenever those views conflict with their own.
  4. (D)show that the line-judgement experiments were badly designed, since a room arranged to mislead participants cannot reveal anything about behaviour in everyday life.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 states the familiar gloomy moral, paragraphs 2 and 3 challenge it (most answers were correct; yielding often reflected 'treating others as evidence'), paragraph 4 concedes that 'Normative pressure ... was real' but only 'part of the picture', and paragraph 5 draws out the consequence for remedies. The correct answer captures both the challenge and the concession.

Why not (B): This reverses the author's emphasis: paragraph 3 makes informational influence central, and paragraph 4 treats normative pressure as 'only part of the picture'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 5 rejects exhorting people 'to be brave' and proposes better information instead; the option also takes the closing remark for the passage's whole purpose.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says the room was 'deliberately arranged to defeat' a sound rule, but the author uses this to reinterpret the results, not to dismiss the experiments as uninformative.

CAT-VARC-P02-034 · Social psychology · passage Easy · 430 words · 4 questions

Few findings in social psychology have escaped the laboratory as completely as the bystander effect. In its popular form it says that the more people witness an emergency, the less likely the victim is to receive help, and it is usually offered as evidence of modern apathy: crowds of onlookers, each waiting for someone else to act. The idea owes much of its fame to a newspaper account of a murder in a city street in the 1960s, in which dozens of neighbours were said to have watched and done nothing. Later investigation suggested that the account greatly exaggerated both the number of witnesses and their indifference, but by then the story had become a parable.

The experiments that followed were more careful than the parable. Their central claim was not that people are callous but that responsibility, when shared, is diluted. A person who believes that she alone has heard a cry for help knows that if she does not act, no one will; a person who knows that others have heard it can reasonably suppose that someone better placed is already responding. Ambiguity compounds the effect. In a group, each witness looks to the others to work out whether anything is really wrong, and the calm faces of people who are themselves only watching can persuade everyone that nothing is.

Yet there is a gap between what these studies measured and what the popular version claims. They showed that a given individual is less likely to help when others are present. They did not show that a victim is less likely to be helped. The two come apart because a crowd contains many potential helpers: even if each one is somewhat less willing, the chance that at least one of them steps forward can remain high, and may rise as the crowd grows. Some recent studies of recorded conflicts in public places have reported that in most of them at least one bystander intervened, a pattern hard to reconcile with the image of the paralysed crowd.

None of this makes the original research worthless. The mechanisms it identified are real, and they help explain why emergencies in which no one is sure what is happening, or whose job it is to act, can go unanswered. What it does not support is a sweeping verdict on human nature. The practical advice that follows from the research is modest and useful: if you need help, single out one person and tell that person what to do, so that responsibility is no longer shared and the situation is no longer ambiguous.

  3. (C)onlookers in a large crowd find it physically harder to see clearly what is happening to the victim.
  4. (D)each witness reads the calm of the other onlookers as a sign that nothing serious is happening.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 attributes the assumption that 'someone better placed is already responding' to shared responsibility, not to ambiguity, and it mentions no authorities.

Why not (B): The passage says the reluctance comes from concluding that nothing is wrong, not from an unwillingness to help people whose situation is unclear.

Why not (C): Physical visibility is never mentioned; paragraph 2 locates the problem in what witnesses infer from 'the calm faces' of others.

Common misconception: Mixing up the two mechanisms in paragraph 2, diffused responsibility and ambiguity.

Difficulty: Located in two sentences of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-035 · Social psychology · passage Medium · 491 words · 4 questions

Imagine being told, after a brief task in which you estimated the number of dots flashed on a screen, that you belong to the group of 'overestimators'. You never meet another member of your group; you do not know who they are; nothing hangs on the label. You are then asked to divide small sums of money between two anonymous people, one described as an overestimator and the other as an underestimator. Experiments built on this design, which came to be called the minimal group paradigm, found that people reliably gave a little more to members of their own arbitrary category. Some even preferred allocations that maximised the gap between the groups over allocations that would have given their own group more in absolute terms.

The result has often been read as a revelation about human nature. If a meaningless label is enough to produce favouritism, the argument runs, then tribalism must be wired into us, waiting for any pretext, and the deeper divisions of nation, religion and ethnicity are only this primitive tendency writ large. The reading is dramatic, but it outruns the evidence in two ways.

First, what the experiments mostly showed was favouritism, not hostility. Participants tilted rewards towards their own side, but they were generally reluctant to impose costs on the other side when the task was framed as distributing penalties rather than rewards. Giving one's own a little more is not the same as wishing the others harm, and a theory of intergroup conflict that treats the two as interchangeable will misunderstand both. Second, the favouritism itself may be less mindless than it looks. Several later studies suggested that it shrinks when participants are told that members of their group will not know how they allocated, as though people were partly acting on an expectation that their own side would reciprocate. On that account the label is not a trigger for blind loyalty but a cue about whom one is in a cooperative relationship with.

None of this makes the original finding trivial. It remains striking that so thin a basis for belonging should shape behaviour at all, and the speed with which people adopt a category as their own deserves to be taken seriously. But the lesson that follows is not the fatalistic one. If group membership can be conjured from a coin toss or a dot-counting task, then it can also be redrawn: categories that are learned so easily are not fixed, and identities that cut across existing divisions can be built by the same mechanism that builds divisions in the first place.

What the laboratory cannot tell us is why particular divisions in the world become bitter. Real groups have histories, grievances and competing claims on land, jobs and power, and a five-minute label carries none of these. The minimal group paradigm shows where the raw material of loyalty comes from; it does not show what turns that material into enmity.

  3. (C)gave more to the other group when they believed that members of their own group were watching their choices.
  4. (D)refused to give any money at all to members of the other group once they learned which group they belonged to.

Paragraph 1: 'Some even preferred allocations that maximised the gap between the groups over allocations that would have given their own group more in absolute terms.'

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 reports that favouritism shrinks when one's group will not know the allocations; it never says observation led people to favour the other group.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 says participants gave 'a little more' to their own group, not that they gave nothing to the other group.

Common misconception: Inflating mild favouritism into outright exclusion.

Difficulty: Stated in the last sentence of paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-035-Q2 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ
