CAT-VARC-P02-036 · Social psychology · passage Medium · 480 words · 4 questions

Empathy enjoys a reputation that few other psychological capacities can match. To be told that one lacks it is an accusation; to call for more of it is to sound wise. Yet if empathy is understood in its strict sense, as the capacity to feel what another person feels, to be distressed by their distress and buoyed by their relief, there are reasons to doubt that it is a dependable guide to how we ought to act, however much it enriches how we live.

The trouble lies in its shape. Empathy works like a narrow beam: it illuminates whatever it is pointed at with great intensity and leaves everything else in darkness. It is most easily directed at people who are near us, who resemble us and who can be seen as individuals with faces and names. It is notoriously insensitive to number; the plight of one child described in detail can move us more than a report of thousands suffering in the same way, because thousands cannot be felt, only counted. A capacity with these properties will reliably favour the identifiable over the statistical, the familiar over the strange and the present over the future, and those are precisely the biases that moral reasoning is supposed to correct.

There is a darker possibility as well. Because empathy is felt for particular victims, it can be recruited against other people. An account of a single person wronged by a member of some group can stir a fierce sympathy that is then turned into demands for punishment of the whole group. The emotion in such cases is genuine; what is wrong is its range.

The obvious objection is that without empathy we would not care at all, and that a morality built only on principle would be bloodless. But this assumes that empathy is the only route to concern. Many people help strangers without imagining their way into the strangers' experience, out of a settled wish that others fare well, a sense of fairness, or the plain recognition that suffering is bad wherever it occurs. Psychologists sometimes distinguish empathy from compassion along these lines: the first means sharing another's feeling, the second means caring about another's good without necessarily sharing the feeling. Compassion, unlike empathic distress, does not exhaust the carer, and it can be extended to people one will never see.

None of this is an argument for coldness. In friendship, in parenting and in the consulting room, the ability to feel with another person is part of what makes the relationship what it is, and nobody would wish it away. The claim is narrower: that when we must decide how to allocate help across many people, or how to treat those we do not know, a feeling that shines only where it is pointed should be checked by judgement rather than trusted as the measure of what is right.

CAT-VARC-P02-036-Q1 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ

  3. (C)the feeling is real, but the selective set of people it reaches and excludes is what makes it harmful.
  4. (D)the feeling is not truly felt, but is a pretence adopted in order to justify punishing members of a group.

  2. (B)favour the present over the future when people weigh whose needs to attend to.

  4. (D)respond more strongly to a single identifiable victim than to large numbers suffering in the same way.

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says empathy favours 'the familiar over the strange' and is most easily directed at people 'who are near us, who resemble us'. A tendency to weaken with familiarity is the reverse of what the passage says, so it is the exception.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 lists 'the present over the future' among the biases empathy reliably produces.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 describes exactly this: sympathy 'turned into demands for punishment of the whole group'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the plight of one child 'can move us more than a report of thousands', so this is attributed to empathy.

CAT-VARC-P02-037 · Social psychology · passage Medium · 473 words · 4 questions

Why should anyone help a stranger they will never meet again? Kindness between relatives can be explained by shared interests, and kindness between neighbours by the expectation that favours will be returned. But much of human cooperation takes place between people who are neither kin nor repeat partners: the traveller who returns a lost wallet, the customer who corrects an undercharge. One influential answer is that such acts are paid for not by the person helped but by everyone who comes to hear of them. If people prefer to help those who have a record of helping others, then a good reputation is an asset, and an act of generosity towards a stranger is an investment in it. Biologists call this arrangement indirect reciprocity.

The idea has an obvious weakness, and its defenders have had to work hard to answer it. Reputation is only as useful as the information that carries it. In a small band where everyone watches everyone, deeds are seen and remembered; in a large society most of them go unwitnessed. Here language does decisive work. Gossip, often dismissed as idle, is the channel through which a person's conduct becomes known to people who never saw it, and a great deal of everyday conversation consists of exactly this exchange of judgements about absent third parties. Without such talk, indirect reciprocity could hardly operate beyond the scale of a village.

A subtler problem concerns the judgements themselves. Suppose I refuse to help someone who is known to cheat. Should observers count my refusal against me? If they do, anyone who withholds help from cheats will be punished for it, and cheating becomes safer. If they do not, observers must keep track not only of what people did but of whom they did it to and why. Models of indirect reciprocity suggest that cooperation is far more stable under the second, more demanding standard, which may help explain why people care so much about motives and context when they assess one another's behaviour.

It would be too much to say that reputation explains all cooperation among strangers. People sometimes behave generously in conditions of genuine anonymity, and laboratory attempts to show that subtle cues of being watched, such as pictures of eyes, make people more generous have produced mixed results. A defender of the reputational account can reply that habits formed in a world where anonymity was rare are not switched off merely because a particular situation happens to be unobserved. That reply is plausible, but it shifts the explanation from calculation to disposition, and it should be acknowledged as a shift. What survives is a narrower and more defensible claim: that the expectation of being talked about is one of the main forces that make large-scale cooperation possible, and that it works only as well as the talk is accurate.

CAT-VARC-P02-038 · Social psychology · passage Medium · 496 words · 4 questions

For a time it was widely believed, among journalists and fact-checkers as much as among researchers, that correcting a false belief could make matters worse. The idea, which came to be called the backfire effect, held that people confronted with evidence against a cherished belief would not merely resist it but cling to the belief more tightly, as though the correction were an attack to be repelled. It had an intuitive appeal: most of us have argued with someone who seemed to grow more certain with every fact we offered. And it carried a discouraging moral. If correction backfires, perhaps it is better not to correct at all.

Subsequent research has been considerably kinder to correction. Larger studies, with more varied topics and more diverse participants, have generally found that people who receive a factual correction move, on average, towards the accurate view, even when the correction cuts against their political loyalties. Reversal of the kind the early findings described appears to be rare, and some of the original results have proved difficult to reproduce. The more careful conclusion is not that people are perfectly responsive to evidence but that they are responsive to it in the ordinary, imperfect way: they update, though often less than they should.

It is worth asking why the backfire idea spread so far on so slender a basis. Part of the answer is that it flattered a certain cynicism about the public, and part is that it confused resistance with reversal. Someone who hears a correction and does not change his mind has resisted it; someone who becomes more convinced of the falsehood has reversed. The first is common and unremarkable, since people rarely abandon a belief after a single encounter with contrary evidence. Only the second would justify the conclusion that correcting is counterproductive, and it is the second that the evidence has failed to find with any regularity.

This does not mean that correction is a cure. Its effects often fade over weeks, so that people drift back towards the belief they were talked out of, and accepting that a particular claim is false does not necessarily change the attitudes the claim was used to support. A voter may concede that a politician's statistic was wrong and still admire the politician exactly as much. These are serious limits, but they are limits of a different kind. They call for corrections that are repeated, that offer an alternative explanation rather than a bare denial, and that are not expected to carry more weight than a single fact can bear. They do not call for silence.

The history of the backfire effect is thus a small lesson about the life of findings as well as about belief. A striking result, especially one that confirms what people already suspect, can travel much faster than the slower work of checking it, and the effort to correct a mistaken finding meets the same ordinary resistance, though not reversal, that greets any correction.

CAT-VARC-P02-038-Q1 · Inference · MediumMCQ

  1. (A)shows that the larger studies described in paragraph 2 were mistaken about how people respond to corrections.
  2. (B)demonstrates that people are perfectly unresponsive to evidence whenever a belief is tied to their loyalties.
  3. (C)offers no support for the view that correcting a false belief is likely to do more harm than good.
  4. (D)is an example of the backfire effect, since the correction has failed to weaken the belief at all.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says that 'Only the second', reversal, 'would justify the conclusion that correcting is counterproductive'. Unchanged confidence is resistance, which is 'common and unremarkable', so it gives no support to that conclusion.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 reports average movement towards accuracy and imperfect updating; one person's resistance is compatible with that finding.

Why not (B): The passage says people update 'though often less than they should', and that resistance after 'a single encounter' is 'unremarkable'; calling people perfectly unresponsive is extreme.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 defines this as resistance, not reversal; backfire would require the person to become 'more convinced of the falsehood'.

Common misconception: Treating any failure of a correction as a backfire, which is exactly the confusion paragraph 3 diagnoses.

Difficulty: Requires applying the resistance and reversal distinction to a case rather than recalling it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-039 · Social psychology · passage Difficult · 608 words · 5 questions

In most human groups some people are listened to more than others, get their way more often and receive a larger share of whatever the group values. Students of status have long suspected that this single word conceals two different arrangements. In one, a person rises because others fear him: he can hurt them, withhold what they need or make their lives difficult, and they defer in order to avoid those costs. In the other, a person rises because others want something from him that he gives freely, usually knowledge or skill, and they defer in order to stay close enough to learn. The first route is commonly called dominance and the second prestige, and an influential line of thought in evolutionary anthropology holds that the second is a distinctively human addition to an older primate inheritance.

The argument for that view rests on the peculiar importance of cultural learning in our species. A creature that acquires most of its survival skills by copying others faces a constant problem of whom to copy, and a strong incentive to secure access to the best models. Deference, on this account, is a kind of payment: the learner offers attention, praise, small services and a willingness to yield in disputes, and in return is tolerated at the elbow of the skilled. If the account is right, it explains why prestige is freely conferred rather than extracted, why prestigious people are approached rather than avoided, and why we find ourselves imitating not only what admired people do well but much else about them besides.

That last consequence deserves more attention than it usually receives, because it shows that prestige is not simply the benign twin of dominance. Imitation guided by prestige is broad and indiscriminate. A learner cannot always tell which of a successful person's habits are responsible for the success, and so tends to copy a bundle of them: the method, but also the dress, the opinions and the manner of speech. In a small community this overspill is cheap and may even be useful, since the bundle often travels together for good reasons. In a large society with mass media, it allows people who are prestigious in one domain to exert influence far outside it, and the deference they receive bears no relation to any competence they have in the matters on which they are now heeded.

There is a further complication. The two routes are clean in theory and tangled in practice. A person who gains prestige acquires allies, resources and a following, and these are exactly the instruments with which dominance is exercised; prestige can thus be converted into the power to coerce, and the conversion is not always visible to those who continue to admire. Conversely, a dominant figure may cultivate the outward marks of prestige, surrounding himself with displays of learning or generosity that make submission feel like admiration. Observers who ask simply whether a leader is feared or respected will often find that the honest answer is both.

None of this undermines the distinction; it is precisely because the two routes can be told apart in principle that their mixture in practice becomes something to look for. What it undermines is the comfortable assumption that a society which replaces dominance with prestige has thereby solved the problem of status. Prestige allocates influence according to perceived excellence, and perception can be managed. The practical question for any institution is therefore not whether it rewards prestige rather than force, which most modern institutions at least claim to do, but whether it keeps the deference people receive tied to the domain in which they have actually earned it.

CAT-VARC-P02-040 · Social psychology · passage Difficult · 599 words · 5 questions

Few episodes have shaken social psychology as much as the attempts, over roughly the past decade and a half, to repeat some of its best-known experiments. Among the findings that fared worst were studies of so-called behavioural priming, which reported that subtle exposure to an idea could shift what people did shortly afterwards: that reading words associated with old age made people walk more slowly down a corridor, for instance, or that being reminded of money made them less helpful. Such results were taught in lecture halls and retold in popular books. When larger and more carefully controlled attempts to reproduce several of them found effects much smaller than those first reported, or none that could be detected, the reaction within the field was bitter, and the debate quickly took on the character of a trial.

That framing has not served anyone well. It invites the question of who was dishonest, when the more troubling lesson is that dishonesty was largely unnecessary. A literature can come to look robust through practices that most of the people using them regarded as normal: running small studies, trying several reasonable ways of analysing the data and reporting the one that worked, and publishing successes while filing failures away. Each practice, taken alone, seems a minor licence. Together they make it likely that a field will accumulate striking positive results whether or not the underlying effects are real, and that the most surprising results, which are the most likely to be false, will also be the most celebrated.

It is important to be clear about what the failed replications do and do not show. They do not show that priming in general is an illusion. That encountering one word makes a related word easier to recognise a moment later is among the most reliable findings in the study of cognition, and nothing in the recent debate casts doubt on it. Nor does a failed repetition of a single study prove that the effect it sought is exactly zero; it shows that the effect, if it exists, is smaller or less dependable than the original report implied. The questions under dispute concern a particular, ambitious claim: that brief and incidental cues reliably steer complex social behaviour.

Defenders of that claim have often replied that social behaviour is sensitive to context, so that an effect obtained with one population, in one decade, with one set of materials, need not appear when any of these is changed. There is something to this. Human beings are not inert objects, and it would be strange if the meaning of a word list were identical across cultures and generations. But the reply has a cost that its proponents do not always acknowledge. The original studies were presented as revealing general features of human minds, not as local facts about particular groups of students. If the effects are so delicate that unknown features of a setting can abolish them, then those general claims were overstated, and an appeal to hidden moderators that is made only after a failure, and never specified in advance so that it could be tested, explains everything and therefore nothing.

The reforms that the episode prompted, such as registering hypotheses and analyses before collecting data and running studies large enough to detect modest effects, have costs of their own. Research becomes slower and more expensive, and fewer dazzling results will appear. That is the right trade. A science of social behaviour that publishes fewer surprises, each of which can bear weight, is worth more than one whose surprises must all be taken on trust.

CAT-VARC-P02-041 · Minds in nature · passage Easy · 465 words · 4 questions

Ask someone to describe the route from their home to the nearest railway station and watch their hands. Before they have finished the first sentence, the hands will usually be tracing turns, marking distances and pointing at landmarks that are nowhere in the room. The common explanation is that gesture is a kind of visual aid for the listener, a way of adding pictures to words. On this view the hands are an accessory to communication, useful when they help an audience and idle otherwise. The explanation is not wrong, but it is incomplete, and the part it leaves out is the more interesting one.

If gesture existed only for listeners, people would stop gesturing when nobody could see them, and yet they do not: speakers on the telephone continue to move their hands, if somewhat less than they would face to face, and people who have been blind from birth gesture when they talk, even to listeners they know to be blind. A habit that persists when it can no longer inform anyone is unlikely to be explained entirely by what it does for others. The more economical conclusion is that gesture also does something for the person who produces it.

What it seems to do is lighten the work of thinking, for when people are asked to explain a problem while keeping their hands still, many speak more haltingly and struggle to find the words they want, particularly words for shapes, directions and positions. Children explaining how they solved a sum sometimes show with their hands a strategy that their words have not yet reached, pointing to two numbers together while describing a different procedure aloud. Such children are often found to be on the verge of learning the correct method, as if the hands had arrived at an idea before speech could carry it. Gesture, on this account, is not a translation of finished thought into movement; it is one of the places where thought is still being worked out.

It would be easy to overstate the case, since not every flourish of the hands is cognitive labour; some gestures are plainly social, like a wave or a shrug, and some speakers gesture little without thinking any worse for it. Nor does the evidence show that thought depends on the body in some mysterious way that rules out abstract reasoning. The claim is more modest: that for many people, much of the time, moving the hands is part of how a difficult idea gets held in mind. Even that modest claim has practical consequences, because a teacher who attends only to what a pupil says may miss what the pupil is close to understanding, and a classroom that rewards stillness may be quietly removing a tool that some children use to think.

  3. (C)argue that gesture serves the speaker's own thinking as well as the listener's understanding.
  4. (D)show that gesture contributes more to successful communication than the spoken words it accompanies.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 calls the listener-only explanation 'incomplete', paragraph 2 argues that gesture 'also does something for the person who produces it', paragraph 3 says it lightens 'the work of thinking', and paragraph 4 restates the modest version of this claim. The purpose is to add the speaker's thinking to the familiar communicative role.

Why not (A): The blind speakers appear in paragraph 2 as one piece of evidence; the option takes a supporting example for the purpose of the whole passage.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says only that a classroom rewarding stillness 'may be quietly removing a tool'; it notes a consequence and makes no demand that rules be dropped.

Why not (D): The passage never ranks gesture above speech; paragraph 1 grants that gesture helps listeners and then adds a second function, so the comparison is invented.

Common misconception: Choosing the classroom point because it comes at the end, or overstating the thesis into a ranking of gesture above speech.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated almost directly in paragraphs 1 and 2; wrong options are a detail, an exaggeration and a prescription. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-042 · Minds in nature · passage Easy · 425 words · 4 questions

When people imagine an intelligent animal, they usually imagine something built on the plan of their own bodies: a large brain in the head that gathers information, makes decisions and issues commands to obedient limbs. The octopus is a standing challenge to that picture. Its evolutionary line separated from ours more than half a billion years ago, long before either lineage had anything resembling a complex brain, so whatever intelligence it now has was assembled independently. And it was assembled on a strikingly different plan, for more of the octopus's neurons lie in its arms than in its central brain.

The arrangement has consequences that can be observed. An octopus arm can carry out a sequence of movements, such as reaching towards an object and bending to draw it in, with little apparent direction from the centre, and an arm separated from the body will for a time continue to respond to touch and to grasp what it meets. Each arm, it seems, handles much of the detail of its own movement, while the central brain sets broader goals. Instead of a commander and its troops, the better image might be a coordinator working with partners that each know their own business.

Popular writing has been quick to turn this into a slogan, describing the octopus as an animal with nine brains, or as a colony of minds sharing one body. The slogan goes too far. The arms are not independent agents with purposes of their own; they share a body, a nervous system and, in ordinary life, a single course of action, and the animal behaves as one creature pursuing one end at a time. What the anatomy shows is not that the octopus is many minds but that one mind can be organised so that much of its work is done at the edges.

That is the real lesson, and it reaches beyond octopuses. Much thinking about intelligence, in animals and in machines alike, has assumed that it must be centralised: that there must be some single place where everything comes together and is decided. The octopus suggests that centralisation is one solution among several, suited to some bodies and not to others. An animal with eight flexible arms and no skeleton faces a control problem that no single centre could easily solve, since each arm can bend at any point along its length and in any direction. Handing much of the work to the arms may be less an evolutionary curiosity than a sensible answer to the body the animal actually has.

  3. (C)the octopus has a far larger share of its neurons in its central brain than vertebrates have in theirs.

Common misconception: Linking 'independently' to the arms or to solitary learning rather than to the evolutionary split stated in the same sentence.

Difficulty: The reason is given in the same sentence as the quoted phrase. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-043 · Minds in nature · passage Medium · 437 words · 4 questions

The mirror test has an appealing simplicity: an animal is given a mark it cannot see directly, perhaps a spot of odourless dye on the forehead, and is then shown a mirror. If it uses the reflection to inspect or rub at the mark on its own body, it is said to recognise itself. Great apes generally pass, most monkeys generally fail, and over the decades a handful of other species have been reported to pass or nearly pass. Because the result is so easy to state, it has come to be treated as a kind of membership test: those who pass belong among the self-aware, and those who fail are presumed to lack a self worth the name.

The test does measure something real. To pass, an animal must grasp that the moving figure in the glass is not another individual and that what it sees there corresponds to its own body. That is not a trivial achievement, and it is reasonable to think that animals capable of it have some representation of their own bodies as objects among others. The difficulty lies in what is inferred from failure, and in how much is read into success.

Consider failure first, since the test presupposes an animal that relies heavily on vision, that attends to its own appearance, and that is bothered enough by an odd mark to investigate it. Many animals meet none of these conditions. A dog, which navigates its world largely by smell, may simply have no interest in a silent image that gives off no scent; an animal whose species does not groom may see no reason to touch a spot on its skin. Failing a test designed around one animal's priorities is weak evidence that another animal lacks the capacity being tested, in the way that a person's failure to answer a question asked in an unfamiliar language reveals little about her knowledge.

Success is open to over-reading too. Recognising one's body in a mirror shows an understanding of the body as one's own; it does not show, by itself, that the animal reflects on its past, imagines its future or thinks of itself as a person with a history. These are richer capacities, and the leap from the mirror to them is a leap of interpretation, not of evidence. None of this makes the test worthless. It makes it one probe among many, informative within its range and silent outside it. The error is not in running the test but in letting a single, visually biased procedure decide which minds are admitted to the category of the self-aware.

  2. (B)That the figure in the mirror is not another animal but corresponds to its own body.
  3. (C)That the mark on its forehead was placed there by the researchers who are running the test.
  4. (D)That its past and its future belong to one continuing individual with a history of its own.
Answer and explanation

Paragraph 2: 'To pass, an animal must grasp that the moving figure in the glass is not another individual and that what it sees there corresponds to its own body.'

Why not (A): Nothing in the passage requires the animal to understand the optics of mirrors; paragraph 2 concerns recognising the reflection as its own body.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 lists what passing requires, and knowing who placed the mark is not part of it; the animal need only connect the reflected mark with its own body.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says that passing does not, by itself, show this richer capacity; it is precisely what the passage says should not be read into success.

Common misconception: Reading the richer capacities of paragraph 4 back into the requirements of the test.

Difficulty: Located in a single sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Paragraph 3 argues that the test presupposes a visual animal that cares about its appearance, so failing a test designed around 'one animal's priorities is weak evidence' about another. The unfamiliar language case is a parallel: failure there shows the format did not fit, not that knowledge is absent.

Why not (A): The analogy is about a mismatch between test and subject, not a claim that language underlies self-recognition; the passage says great apes generally pass and nowhere ties passing the test to language.

Why not (B): The passage criticises the inference drawn from failure, not researchers' care in carrying out the procedure; carelessness is never alleged.

Why not (D): The analogy concerns what failure reveals, not a prediction that any animal would pass a better version; the author never claims to know how failing animals would do.

Common misconception: Turning an argument about weak evidence into a confident prediction that the animals would pass.

Difficulty: The wrong options each misread the analogy in a different plausible way; the correct answer requires seeing it as a point about evidence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-044 · Minds in nature · passage Medium · 467 words · 4 questions

It has become a commonplace that every age describes the brain in the image of its most impressive machine. When ornamental gardens were fitted with water-driven statues that moved as visitors stepped on hidden plates, nerves were pictured as pipes and the animal spirits as fluid pressed through them. When the telegraph and then the telephone exchange spread across cities, the nervous system became a network of wires, with the brain as the central office where calls were connected. In our own time it is a computer, processing information, storing memories and running programs. The pattern is real, and critics have drawn a sharp lesson from it: the metaphors are fashions, each as quaint as the last will soon appear, and the computer image will go the way of the fountain.

The lesson is half right. It is true that none of these images was the whole truth, and that each looks dated once the technology behind it is familiar. But to call them mere fashions is to miss what each one got right. The hydraulic picture captured the idea that something must travel from the senses to the centre and back to the muscles. The switchboard captured the importance of connections, of which signal is routed to which destination. The computer captured the idea that what matters about these signals is the information they carry and the transformations performed on it, rather than the stuff that carries them. Each metaphor, in other words, was adopted because it made some feature of the nervous system thinkable, and each left behind a contribution that survived its own retirement.

The real danger lies elsewhere, because a metaphor brings with it not only the features that fit but a whole set of features that come along unexamined. The computer image invites us to think of memory as storage, a place where a record sits unchanged until retrieved, when remembering appears to be closer to reconstruction, the record altered a little each time it is used. It invites a sharp division between hardware and software, when in the brain the program and the machine are hard to tell apart, since use reshapes the very structures that do the work. These are not reasons to abandon the metaphor but reasons to keep asking which of its features we are actually entitled to.

The sensible course, then, is neither to treat the current metaphor as a literal description nor to dismiss it as a passing fashion, but to use it while keeping an account of where it fails. Metaphors are tools for thinking about something that cannot be seen directly, and the history of brain science suggests that thinking without any of them is not an available option. What can be avoided is the habit of mistaking the tool for the thing.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 opens 'The lesson is half right', so the author does not agree completely, and denies that the old images were empty.

Why not (D): The author spends paragraph 2 answering the critics, which shows the question matters to the argument.

Common misconception: Missing the signal phrase 'half right' and choosing a whole-hearted position.

Difficulty: The attitude is named in a short opening sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-045 · Minds in nature · passage Medium · 452 words · 4 questions

Among the people who first tried to build thinking machines, a surprise emerged that has never quite been absorbed. The tasks that seemed to demand the most intelligence, such as proving theorems, playing chess or solving problems in algebra, turned out to be comparatively tractable for computers, while tasks that any small child performs without effort, such as recognising a face across a room, picking up an unfamiliar object or walking over uneven ground, proved stubbornly hard. The observation is often associated with the roboticist Hans Moravec, and it is usually called a paradox. Whether it deserves the name depends on what we took difficulty to be in the first place.

The most common explanation is evolutionary. Seeing, grasping and moving have been refined over hundreds of millions of years of animal life, and the machinery that performs them is vast, ancient and almost entirely hidden from awareness. Abstract reasoning, by contrast, is a recent arrival, practised deliberately and with effort. We notice the effort and conclude that reasoning is hard; we notice nothing when we recognise a friend's face, and conclude that perception is easy. On this account the paradox is an artefact of introspection: our sense of difficulty tracks how much of a skill reaches consciousness, not how much computation it requires.

This explanation is persuasive, but it should not be stretched into the claim that every skill we find effortless is computationally deep, or that every skill we find laborious is shallow. Some effortless skills may be easy for machines too, and some laborious ones, like long division, are trivial for any pocket calculator while others remain hard. The evolutionary story explains a general tendency; it does not supply a rule. Nor has the gap stood still. Machines have become far better at recognising images and speech than early researchers expected, though a robot that can fold an unfamiliar garment or clear an untidy table remains much harder to build than a program that plays chess at a master's level.

The deeper lesson concerns our idea of intelligence itself. For a long time intelligence was identified with the activities that educated adults found hardest and prized most: calculation, logic, formal games. The paradox suggests that this identification was parochial. What humans find hard is a measure of human limitations, not a measure of how much intelligence a task needs, and the competences we share with other animals may contain more of what is difficult about minds than the competences that distinguish us. A cat crossing a cluttered shelf without disturbing anything is solving problems that our best machines still find formidable, and it would be odd to call that achievement unintelligent merely because no cat could sit an examination.

  3. (C)the processes that carry it out operate almost entirely outside conscious awareness.
  4. (D)it is performed by a small and specialised part of the brain that works without needing effort.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 calls the machinery of perception 'vast, ancient and almost entirely hidden from awareness'; nothing in it describes perception as the work of a small brain region.

Common misconception: Confusing seeming easy with being computationally light, which the passage explicitly separates.

Difficulty: Stated directly in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-046 · Minds in nature · passage Difficult · 474 words · 4 questions

The claim that language shapes thought has had an unusual career. In its strong form, the view that the categories of one's language set the limits of what one can perceive or conceive, it was once fashionable, then thoroughly discredited, and it is now defended by almost nobody. The objections are familiar. Speakers of languages that lack a word for some distinction can usually learn to make it when they need to; translation, though imperfect, is possible; and people routinely think thoughts for which they struggle to find words. If a language were a prison, its inmates would not so regularly be found outside the walls.

From the ruins of the strong view a weaker one has been rebuilt, and the evidence for it is more respectable. Colour has been the favoured test case, because the spectrum is continuous while languages divide it differently: some have only a few basic colour terms, others a dozen. In one well-known kind of experiment, speakers of a language that has separate basic words for lighter and darker blue were found to be slightly quicker at telling apart two blues that fell on opposite sides of that boundary than two blues on the same side, while speakers of a language with a single basic word for blue showed no such advantage. The effect was modest, but it was there, and it seemed to show that words reach down into perception.

What the same line of work also found, however, is more revealing than the effect itself. When participants performed the colour task while silently rehearsing a string of digits, which occupies the verbal system, the advantage largely disappeared, whereas a comparable task that occupied spatial memory instead left it intact. The words, it seems, were not permanently altering how the visual system sorted colours. They were being used, there and then, as an aid to the task, recruited on the spot and set aside when the verbal system was busy elsewhere.

Defenders and critics of linguistic relativity have both tended to miss what this implies. The defenders read such results as vindication: language shapes thought after all. The critics read the smallness of the effects as deflation: language barely matters. But the finding fits neither reading well. It suggests that language does not so much reshape a mind that would otherwise be neutral as serve as one of the instruments a mind reaches for while it works. On this picture the interesting question is not whether a speaker's language has engraved its categories on her perception, but how often, and in which tasks, thinking proceeds by borrowing words as tools. That question is in one respect more modest than the relativists' and in another more radical, since it implies that much ordinary thought is partly carried out in language even where we take it to be wordless.

  2. (B)suggest that translation between languages, however carefully attempted, is never fully possible.
  3. (C)sum up, in a single image, the objections that count against the strong form of the view.
  4. (D)concede that the strong view contains a real, if limited, truth about how language confines its speakers.

Answer: (C)

Why not (B): The objections say translation 'though imperfect, is possible'; the image is about people escaping the limits of their language, which is the reverse of this option.

Why not (D): The paragraph calls the strong view 'thoroughly discredited'; the image is a reductio of the view, not a concession to it.

Common misconception: Reading the final sentence as a transition to paragraph 2.

CAT-VARC-P02-047 · Minds in nature · passage Medium · 589 words · 5 questions

In the 1890s the British psychologist Conwy Lloyd Morgan proposed a rule for interpreting animal behaviour that has shaped the study of animal minds ever since. In paraphrase, it says that we should not explain an animal's action as the product of a higher mental faculty if it can be explained as the product of a lower one. The rule was a response to a real problem. Much of what then passed for evidence about animal intelligence consisted of anecdotes sent in by pet owners and naturalists, in which dogs reasoned, cats planned and birds grieved, and in which the observer's own feelings were read into whatever the animal happened to do. Against this habit, Morgan's canon was a discipline: before crediting a creature with insight, ask whether trial and error, habit or simple association would account for what you saw.

As a corrective, the canon did its work well. It pushed the field towards controlled observation and towards explanations that could be tested, and it is partly responsible for the expectation that claims about animal cognition must now survive experiments designed to rule out simpler accounts. But a rule that is useful against one error can become an error of its own when it is applied without thought. Over the following century the canon hardened, in many hands, into a presumption that the lowest available explanation is always to be preferred, whatever the animal and whatever the evidence.

The trouble with that presumption is that it is not as neutral as it looks. It treats 'lower' as if it meant 'simpler' and 'simpler' as if it meant 'more probably true', and neither equation holds in general. An explanation in terms of association may require positing a long and improbable history of exactly the right rewards, while an explanation in terms of a modest capacity to represent what another animal can see may be the more economical of the two. Evolution complicates the picture further. If a chimpanzee and a human behave alike in similar circumstances, the assumption that the behaviour rests on similar mechanisms is, given their close kinship, arguably the simpler hypothesis; to insist on a different and lower mechanism for the chimpanzee alone is to multiply explanations, not to reduce them.

There is also a question of cost. The canon guards against one kind of mistake, crediting animals with capacities they lack, and is silent about the opposite mistake, denying them capacities they have. Both mistakes are real, and the second is not obviously the less harmful, especially where the capacities in question include the ability to suffer. An investigator who refuses on principle to credit a dog with fear until every leaner account has been excluded will seldom be caught overstating anything, but she may spend a career understating what is in front of her. A principle that treats one error as serious and the other as negligible is not a principle of caution; it is a bias that happens to look like one.

None of this is a case for returning to the anecdote. Morgan himself appears to have intended his rule as a guard against careless inference rather than as a ban on crediting animals with higher faculties when there is independent evidence for them. The better successor to the canon is not its opposite but a symmetrical standard: state the rival hypotheses, rich and lean alike, and design observations that would tell them apart. Simplicity then becomes something to be argued for in each case, rather than a verdict delivered in advance.

CAT-VARC-P02-047-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, Morgan's canon was first proposed as a response to
  1. (A)a growing belief among scientists that animals were mere machines that lacked any mental faculties at all.
  2. (B)disputes over whether chimpanzees and humans rely on similar mechanisms when they behave in similar ways.
  3. (C)reports of animal intelligence in which observers read their own feelings into what animals did.
  4. (D)experiments on animals that had been designed so poorly that they could not rule out simpler explanations.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1: the evidence of the time 'consisted of anecdotes sent in by pet owners and naturalists ... in which the observer's own feelings were read into whatever the animal happened to do', and the canon was a discipline 'Against this habit'.

Why not (A): The passage describes the opposite excess, anecdotes in which 'dogs reasoned, cats planned and birds grieved'; denial of animal minds is not what Morgan was answering.

Why not (B): The chimpanzee case appears in paragraph 3 as part of the author's critique of the later presumption, not as the problem Morgan addressed.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 says the problem was anecdote, not poor experiments; controlled experiments are presented in paragraph 2 as something the canon later encouraged.

Common misconception: Taking the later consequences of the canon, described in paragraph 2, as its original target.

CAT-VARC-P02-047-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred from paragraph 3 that, when two closely related species behave alike in similar circumstances, the author regards an explanation in terms of shared mechanisms as
  1. (A)certainly correct, since close kinship guarantees that similar behaviour arises from similar mechanisms.
  2. (B)possibly the more parsimonious account, contrary to what the rigid reading of the canon implies.
  3. (C)a kind of anthropomorphism that the canon was rightly designed to prevent scientists from adopting.
  4. (D)impossible to test, since the mechanisms behind behaviour can never be observed directly in either species.

Answer: (B)

Why not (D): Paragraph 5 recommends designing 'observations that would tell them apart', so the author thinks such hypotheses can be tested.

CAT-VARC-P02-047-Q3 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best describes the author's attitude towards Morgan's canon?
  1. (A)Critical of Morgan for having meant his rule as a ban on crediting any animal with a higher faculty.
  2. (B)Hostile, since the author holds that it set the study of animal minds back by encouraging excessive caution.
  3. (C)Approving of the presumption it later became, while regretting that it began as a reaction against anecdote.
  4. (D)Appreciative of what it achieved as a corrective, but critical of the fixed presumption it later hardened into.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The author praises the canon's original role (paragraph 2) and spares Morgan himself (paragraph 5), but argues in paragraphs 3 and 4 that the hardened presumption is 'not as neutral as it looks' and is in effect 'a bias'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 5 says Morgan 'appears to have intended his rule as a guard against careless inference rather than as a ban', so the author does not blame Morgan for the hardened version.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says 'As a corrective, the canon did its work well' and credits it with pushing the field towards testable explanations, which is not hostility.

Why not (C): This reverses the author's view: the author calls the later presumption 'a bias that happens to look like' caution and does not regret the reaction against anecdote.

Common misconception: Treating criticism of the later use as criticism of Morgan's own intention.

Difficulty: Two wrong options confuse the canon's origin with its later use; the reader must keep the two apart. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-047-Q4 · Except / not supported · MediumMCQ
The author criticises the presumption in favour of the lowest explanation on each of the following grounds EXCEPT that it
  1. (A)can require separate explanations for similar behaviour in closely related species, rather than fewer.
  2. (B)relies on experiments that are unable, in principle, to tell lean explanations apart from rich ones.
  3. (C)equates a lower explanation with a simpler one, and a simpler explanation with a more probable one.
  4. (D)guards against crediting animals with capacities they lack while ignoring the error of denying capacities they have.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The author never says that experiments cannot separate lean and rich explanations; on the contrary, paragraph 5 recommends designing 'observations that would tell them apart'. The other three criticisms appear in paragraphs 3 and 4.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says that insisting on a lower mechanism for the chimpanzee alone is 'to multiply explanations, not to reduce them'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 makes exactly this criticism: the presumption takes a lower explanation to be a simpler one, and a simpler one to be 'more probably true'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says the canon 'is silent about the opposite mistake, denying them capacities they have', which is this criticism.

Common misconception: Assuming that because the author criticises the canon, any criticism of animal research must be the author's.

CAT-VARC-P02-047-Q5 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
A researcher wants to know whether crows that hide food take account of which other birds have watched them. Which approach best reflects the 'symmetrical standard' the author recommends?
  1. (A)State both an association account and an account in which crows track what others have seen, and design trials in which the two predict different behaviour.
  2. (B)Design an experiment to test the association account first, and accept the richer account only if the association account clearly fails.
  3. (C)Collect many careful reports from experienced bird-watchers, and accept whichever account the largest number of reports supports.
  4. (D)Accept the account in which crows track what others have seen, since crows are widely regarded as unusually intelligent birds.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 5 describes the symmetrical standard as: 'state the rival hypotheses, rich and lean alike, and design observations that would tell them apart', with simplicity 'argued for in each case, rather than a verdict delivered in advance'. Only the correct answer treats both hypotheses on equal terms and seeks a discriminating test.

Why not (B): Although it uses an experiment, it still makes the lean account the default that must be defeated, which is the asymmetry paragraph 4 calls 'a bias'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 5 says 'None of this is a case for returning to the anecdote', and reports from observers are the kind of evidence the canon was meant to discipline.

Why not (D): This adopts the rich explanation by default, which is the 'opposite' of the canon that paragraph 5 says is not the better successor.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that tests the lean account first, because it sounds rigorous, without noticing that it keeps the lean account as the default.

Difficulty: Two options involve experiments and differ only in whether one hypothesis is privileged; the reader must apply the idea of symmetry exactly. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P02-048 · Minds in nature · passage Difficult · 627 words · 5 questions

Any science of consciousness faces an awkward fact at its foundation. The thing it studies is, by most accounts, directly available only to the person who has it, and the scientist learns about it chiefly through what that person says or otherwise signals: I saw the flash, or I did not; the face looked angry; the tone seemed to come from the left. Two opposite attitudes to this fact have been common. One holds that reports are so unreliable, so shaped by expectation, vocabulary and the wish to please, that a science resting on them is no science at all. The other treats a sincere report as the last word, since no one, it is said, is better placed than the subject to know what she experiences.

Neither attitude survives inspection, and the second is the easier to dismiss. People are often wrong about their own experience in ways that can be demonstrated: they confidently describe detail at the edges of their visual field that is not available to them, and they misjudge the order or timing of events they claim to have perceived. A report is a response produced by the subject, shaped by the question asked and the categories available, and it can misdescribe the experience it is about. But the first attitude draws the wrong conclusion from this. Every scientific instrument can misreport what it measures; the usual response is to study its errors, not to throw it away. A report is best treated as evidence about experience, fallible but informative, to be calibrated against other evidence rather than either revered or ignored.

The most productive methods have used reports in exactly this spirit. In masking experiments, the same image is shown on many trials, sometimes followed so quickly by a second image that the first is not reported as seen. Because the physical stimulus is held constant, differences in brain activity between seen and unseen trials can be attributed, at least tentatively, to the difference in experience rather than to the stimulus. The approach is powerful, but it carries a hidden complication. On seen trials the subject not only experiences the image but also prepares and makes a report about it, so some of the activity that distinguishes the two kinds of trial may belong to reporting rather than to experiencing.

To get around this, researchers have designed experiments in which subjects make no report at all, and conscious states are inferred from other signs, such as involuntary eye movements that track which of two competing images is being experienced. Critics point out a circle here: those signs were themselves validated by showing that they agree with reports. If reports are suspect, so, it seems, are the signs that stand in for them. The objection is clever, but it proves less than it appears to. The history of measurement is full of such circles. Early thermometers were checked against the sensation of warmth and then against one another, and in time they came to correct the very sensations against which they had first been checked. A circle of mutual calibration is vicious only if it never widens; when independent methods, each imperfect, are tested against one another and turn out to agree, confidence in all of them can rise together.

None of this guarantees that a science of consciousness will answer every question asked of it, and some philosophers doubt that any amount of correlating brain activity with reports could explain why experience exists at all. That doubt may be justified. But it is a doubt about the reach of the science, not about whether reports can serve as its data, and running the two together has done more to obscure the field than either the sceptics or the enthusiasts have usually admitted.

CAT-VARC-P02-048-Q1 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The example of early thermometers in paragraph 4 is used to
  1. (A)suggest that the study of consciousness is still at the stage that the study of heat was centuries ago.
  2. (B)argue that no-report methods should replace reports entirely in the study of conscious experience.
  3. (C)show that a circle of mutual checking among imperfect measures need not be a vicious one.
  4. (D)show that bodily sensations are a more trustworthy guide to temperature than thermometers are.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 4 answers the critics' circle with the thermometer history and draws the moral directly: 'A circle of mutual calibration is vicious only if it never widens'.

Why not (A): The comparison concerns the logic of calibration, not the maturity of the two fields; nothing is said about consciousness research being backward.

Why not (B): The example defends no-report methods against the charge of circularity; the author never says reports should be replaced, and treats them as evidence to be calibrated.

Why not (D): The example says the reverse: thermometers 'came to correct the very sensations against which they had first been checked'.

Common misconception: Reading the analogy as a claim about relative maturity of sciences, or as a preference for instruments over reports.

Difficulty: The moral of the example is stated in the sentence after it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-048-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred that, in the masking experiments described in paragraph 3, holding the physical stimulus constant matters because it
  1. (A)shows that subjects' reports of what they have seen are accurate and can be accepted without checking.
  2. (B)rules out differences in the image itself as the cause of differences in brain activity between kinds of trial.
  3. (C)removes any influence that the act of reporting might have on the brain activity that is recorded.
  4. (D)ensures that the subject has actually seen the image on every trial, whatever her report says.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 3: 'Because the physical stimulus is held constant, differences in brain activity between seen and unseen trials can be attributed, at least tentatively, to the difference in experience rather than to the stimulus.'

Why not (A): Holding the stimulus fixed does nothing to validate reports, and paragraph 2 says reports must be 'calibrated against other evidence'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the reverse: even with the stimulus held constant, some activity 'may belong to reporting rather than to experiencing'.

Why not (D): The method depends on the image sometimes not being reported as seen; constancy of the stimulus does not make every trial a seen trial.

Common misconception: Thinking the control also removes the reporting confound, which the next sentences say it does not.

Difficulty: Requires seeing what the control does and, from the following sentences, what it does not do. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P02-048-Q3 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author would most likely say that a subject's sincere report that she saw nothing on a particular trial
  1. (A)is less reliable than the involuntary eye movements that are used to track which image is being experienced.
  2. (B)should be set aside, since reports are shaped too strongly by expectation and the wish to please to count as data.
  3. (C)settles that she had no experience of the image on that trial, since no one else is better placed to know.
  4. (D)is evidence about her experience that could, in principle, turn out to be mistaken.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 says a report 'can misdescribe the experience it is about' and should be treated 'as evidence about experience, fallible but informative'. A sincere report of seeing nothing is therefore evidence, but not beyond error.

Why not (A): The author never ranks such signs above reports; paragraph 4 notes they were 'validated by showing that they agree with reports' and defends a mutual calibration in which neither is simply superior.

Why not (B): This is the first attitude, and paragraph 2 says it 'draws the wrong conclusion'; reports are 'fallible but informative'.

Why not (C): This is the second attitude of paragraph 1, which paragraph 2 rejects because people 'are often wrong about their own experience'.

Common misconception: Choosing the eye-movement option, which sounds like the methodological progress the passage describes.

Difficulty: Two wrong options are the attitudes the author rejects, and the third misreads the calibration argument as a ranking; the reader must hold the author's middle position precisely. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P02-048-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following most closely parallels the reasoning the author uses to answer the critics in paragraph 4?
  1. (A)A witness's account of an accident is accepted in full because the witness is sincere and had a clear view of what happened.
  2. (B)A measuring tape is thrown away for good once it is found to stretch slightly whenever the weather is hot.
  3. (C)Two independent methods for dating ancient timber, each with known errors, come to be trusted more once they agree across many samples, and each is then used to refine the other.
  4. (D)A new thermometer is trusted because it was made in the same workshop as an older thermometer that has always been reliable.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The author's reply is that 'when independent methods, each imperfect, are tested against one another and turn out to agree, confidence in all of them can rise together', as with thermometers that later corrected the sensations that first checked them. The timber case has the same structure: independent, imperfect methods, agreement, and mutual refinement.

Why not (A): This treats a sincere report as the last word, the attitude paragraph 2 rejects; there is no cross-checking of independent methods.

Why not (B): This discards an instrument for being fallible, which is the response paragraph 2 rejects: 'the usual response is to study its errors, not to throw it away'.

Why not (D): This borrows the thermometer but replaces mutual calibration among independent methods with trust in a shared origin, which is not the author's reasoning.

Common misconception: Choosing the thermometer option because it shares surface vocabulary with the passage.

Difficulty: One wrong option copies the passage's example while changing its logic, and the others mirror the two attitudes the author rejects. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P02-048-Q5 · Main idea / primary purpose · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following best states the central argument of the passage?
  1. (A)Reports are fallible evidence that can be calibrated, so doubts about them do not rule out a science of consciousness, whatever its final reach.
  2. (B)Sincere reports may misdescribe detail, but they remain the final authority on what a subject actually experiences.
  3. (C)Because reports so often misdescribe experience, consciousness research should rely on no-report methods instead of on reports.
  4. (D)A science of consciousness will explain why experience exists once its methods of measurement have been calibrated against one another.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 argues that reports are 'fallible but informative' and must be calibrated; paragraphs 3 and 4 show this in practice and answer the circularity objection; paragraph 5 separates doubts about the science's reach from doubts about its data. The correct answer captures both the claim and its limit.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 rejects treating a sincere report as 'the last word'; this option restates the attitude the author dismisses most easily.

Why not (C): No-report signs were 'validated by showing that they agree with reports', and the author defends both as mutually calibrating; the passage does not prefer one to the other.

Why not (D): Paragraph 5 says the doubt about explaining 'why experience exists at all' 'may be justified'; the author does not promise this.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that overstates the science's reach, overlooking the concession in paragraph 5.

Difficulty: Each wrong option captures part of the passage but misstates its scope or reverses a qualification. · Exams: CAT, XAT
